Chapter 1: Introduction to the Antediluvian World  
The Age Before the Flood  
In the beginning, the Creator formed the heavens and the earth, and humanity walked in harmony with the divine order. For generations, the descendants of Adam lived in relative peace, their days numbered in centuries rather than decades. But this golden age could not last. As humanity multiplied and spread across the face of the earth, a darkness crept into the world—not from below, but from above.  
Two hundred angels, known as the Watchers, descended upon Mount Hermon. Bound together by a solemn oath under the leadership of Semyaza, these celestial beings looked upon the daughters of humanity and found them beautiful. Defying their divine station, they took mortal wives and taught forbidden knowledge that would forever corrupt the earth. From these unholy unions came the Nephilim—giants of immense stature and terrible power, "the mighty men who were of old, the men of renown" (Genesis 6:4).  
This is the age of Nephilim Wars.  
A time when bronze swords clash against titanic flesh. When the shadows between stars whisper secrets of forbidden power. When every choice carries eternal weight, and the very air grows heavy with the promise of coming judgment. This is not a world of medieval castles and chivalric codes. This is the dawn of civilization itself—a brutal, beautiful, and terrifying era when humanity stood face-to-face with fallen angels and their monstrous offspring.  
The antediluvian world is one of stark contrasts. Vast wilderness stretches between nascent cities where Cainite metallurgists forge bronze weapons under the watchful eyes of giant overlords. Nomadic tribes wander the wastes, seeking refuge from Nephilim warlords who demand tribute in blood and grain. In hidden enclaves, the righteous line of Seth preserves ancient truths while the Watchers' teachings spread like a plague, tempting mortals with power at the cost of their souls.  
And above it all looms the Flood.  
The Creator has seen the corruption of the earth. The violence of the Nephilim has filled the world with bloodshed. The forbidden arts taught by the Watchers have twisted nature itself. Judgment is coming—not as a distant threat, but as an inevitable tide that even the mightiest giant cannot stand against. In this final age before the Deluge, every soul must choose: Will you stand with the righteous, preserving what little light remains? Will you seek power through forbidden means, embracing the corruption that surrounds you? Or will you carve your own path in a world where the old rules no longer apply?  
Timeline of the Antediluvian Age  
The First Age: Innocence (Years 0–1056 AM)  
From the creation of Adam to the birth of Noah, the world existed in a state of relative purity. Humanity lived in scattered communities, their lifespans stretching for centuries. The descendants of Seth maintained the original faith, calling upon the name of the Most High and preserving the knowledge passed down from Eden. During this time, the world was still recovering from the consequences of the Fall, but hope remained.  
Key Events:  
Year 0: Creation of Adam and Eve  
Year 130: Birth of Seth, the appointed replacement for Abel  
Year 235: Birth of Enosh, when "men began to call upon the name of the LORD" (Genesis 4:26)  
Year 460: Birth of Mahalalel  
Year 622: Birth of Jared  
Year 687: Birth of Enoch, the seventh from Adam  
Year 874: Birth of Methuselah, who would live longer than any other human  
Year 1056: Birth of Noah, whose name means "rest" or "comfort"  
The Second Age: Corruption (Years 1056–1200 AM)  
The descent of the Watchers marks the true beginning of the age of Nephilim Wars. Led by Semyaza, two hundred angels swore an oath on Mount Hermon to take human wives and share celestial knowledge. This was not a random act of rebellion but a calculated decision that would reshape the world forever.  
The Watchers taught humanity arts that were never meant for mortal hands:  
Azazel revealed the secrets of warfare—how to forge swords, craft shields, and create armor. He also taught cosmetics and vanity, turning humanity's focus inward toward physical appearance.  
Semyaza shared enchantments and root-cutting (herbalism and poisons), giving mortals power over life and death through plants.  
Baraqel taught astrology and the observation of stars, opening pathways to divination and fate manipulation.  
Kokabiel revealed the constellations and their hidden meanings.  
Shamsiel showed the signs of the sun.  
Sariel explained the course of the moon.  
Penemue introduced writing and ink, allowing knowledge to be preserved beyond oral tradition.  
Kasdeja taught the darkest arts—abortion and how to strike the embryo in the womb.  
From these unions came the Nephilim, the first generation of giants standing 12 to 15 feet tall. Initially revered as demigods and heroes, they quickly became tyrants. Their appetites were insatiable, consuming resources at an unsustainable rate. When the land could no longer support them, they turned to cannibalism, preying upon the very humanity that had given them birth.  
Key Events:  
Year 1056–1100: The Watchers descend upon Mount Hermon and take human wives  
Year 1100–1150: First generation of Nephilim born; rapid spread of forbidden knowledge  
Year 1150–1200: Nephilim establish dominance over human settlements; cannibalism begins  
The Third Age: Oppression (Years 1200–1500 AM)  
As the first generation of Nephilim aged and died, their spirits could not find rest. Unlike mortal souls, the angelic portion of their nature was immortal. These disembodied spirits became the Refa'im—wandering demons that haunted the living and sought to possess human bodies. Meanwhile, subsequent generations of giants emerged:  
Rephaim: Second-generation giants (9–11 feet tall), associated with death and the underworld  
Anakim: "Long-Necked Ones" (10–13 feet tall), known for their noble bearing and the heavy chains they wore as symbols of status  
Gibborim: "Mighty Men of Old" (6.5–8 feet tall), human-giant hybrids who served as elite warriors and champions  
Horim: Cave-dwelling giant-kin (6–7 feet tall), adapted to underground life with superior darkvision  
Elioud: Third-generation Nephilim (6–7 feet tall), appearing almost human but bearing the diluted blood of giants  
Humanity fractured into distinct lineages:  
Sethites: The righteous line, keepers of the original faith, leading eventually to Noah  
Cainites: Descendants of Cain, city-builders and metallurgists who bore the Mark of Cain  
Wanderers: Nomadic peoples who rejected both the righteous path and the city-builder's way  
Sorcerer Clans: Humans initiated into the Watchers' mysteries, bearing visible marks of their forbidden knowledge  
Cities rose under giant patronage. The first great metropolis, Enoch, was built by Cain's son and named for his grandson. Within its bronze-reinforced walls, the arts of the Watchers flourished. Metallurgy advanced rapidly under Tubal-Cain's guidance. Music filled the air, courtesy of Jubal's innovations. But beneath this veneer of civilization, darkness festered.  
Key Events:  
Year 1200–1300: Rise of Nephilim city-states; establishment of the City of Enoch  
Year 1300–1400: Development of bronze-age technology; spread of Watcher teachings  
Year 1400–1500: Growing oppression of human populations; emergence of resistance movements  
The Fourth Age: Judgment Approaches (Years 1500–1656 AM)  
The corruption of the earth reached its zenith. The Book of Enoch describes the situation with chilling clarity: "And the giants, who were produced from them, devoured the labor of men, and when men could no longer sustain them, the giants turned against them and devoured mankind" (1 Enoch 7:3).  
The Watchers themselves faced divine retribution. The archangels Michael, Gabriel, Raphael, and Uriel were sent to bind the fallen angels and imprison them beneath the earth until the Day of Judgment. Azazel was specifically bound in Dudael, a place of jagged rocks and eternal darkness. But their imprisonment did not end their influence—their teachings lived on, and their offspring continued to dominate the world.  
Prophets arose to warn of coming judgment. Enoch, who "walked with God" (Genesis 5:24), received visions of the Flood and the final judgment of the Watchers. He recorded these revelations in the Book of Watchers, a text that would become crucial for those seeking to understand the true nature of the age. His son Methuselah and grandson Lamech carried this knowledge forward, preparing the way for Noah.  
Meanwhile, the Nephilim grew increasingly desperate. Some sought to build great structures that might withstand the coming waters. Others turned to darker magics, attempting to bind their spirits to the earth so they might survive the Deluge in some form. The Refa'im—disembodied giant spirits—multiplied, possessing humans and spreading chaos wherever they could.  
Key Events:  
Year 1500–1600: Binding of the Watchers; rise of prophetic warnings  
Year 1600–1650: Enoch's translations and prophecies; growing awareness of impending judgment  
Year 1650–1656: Noah receives instructions to build the Ark; final preparations before the Flood  
The Fifth Age: The Deluge (Year 1656 AM)  
"In the six hundredth year of Noah's life, in the second month, on the seventeenth day of the month, on that day all the fountains of the great deep burst forth, and the windows of the heavens were opened" (Genesis 7:11).  
The Flood came not as a natural disaster but as divine judgment. For forty days and forty nights, rain fell upon the earth. The subterranean waters erupted, and the world was submerged. Every living thing that had the breath of life perished—except for Noah, his family, and the creatures preserved in the Ark.  
The Nephilim, for all their might, could not stand against the waters. Their cities were swallowed. Their weapons rusted. Their chains sank into the mud. The Watchers remained bound beneath the earth, awaiting a final judgment that would not come for millennia.  
But the story does not end there.  
The Refa'im—the disembodied spirits of dead Nephilim—could not be drowned. They survived the Flood, becoming the demons and evil spirits that would plague humanity in the ages to come. The teachings of the Watchers persisted in corrupted forms, passed down through secret traditions and hidden texts. And though the antediluvian world was destroyed, its legacy would echo through history, waiting for someone to uncover the truth buried beneath the silt and sediment of the Great Flood.  
Central Themes of Nephilim Wars  
Forbidden Knowledge vs. Divine Wisdom  
At the heart of Nephilim Wars lies a fundamental tension between two sources of power: the forbidden knowledge taught by the Watchers and the divine wisdom preserved by the righteous line. This is not merely a conflict between "magic" and "religion" but a deeper struggle between stolen power and granted grace.  
The Watchers' teachings offer immediate, tangible benefits. Azazel's arts of warfare make warriors nearly invincible. Semyaza's enchantments can charm enemies or poison wells. Baraqel's astrology reveals hidden truths about the future. But every use of this knowledge comes at a cost—literally. The Soul Economy tracks Corruption Points (CP) gained through the use of forbidden arts, representing the spiritual degradation that accompanies such power.  
In contrast, the divine miracles available to Priests and other righteous characters require Righteousness Points (RP), earned through acts of virtue, self-sacrifice, and faithfulness. These powers may seem less flashy than Watcher magic, but they carry no risk of corruption. More importantly, they align the user with the coming judgment rather than against it.  
This theme challenges players to consider the source of their power and the price they are willing to pay. Is it worth gaining the ability to strike down a Nephilim warlord if it means your soul becomes marked for the same judgment? Can you resist the temptation of forbidden knowledge when your allies are dying and only dark magic can save them?  
The Weight of Moral Choice  
Unlike many fantasy RPGs where morality is abstract or inconsequential, Nephilim Wars makes every choice matter—eternally. The Soul Economy is not just a mechanical subsystem but a reflection of the setting's core premise: in the age before the Flood, every soul was being weighed in preparation for judgment.  
Characters begin with a baseline of Righteousness Points (RP) and Corruption Points (CP) based on their lineage. Sethites start with 2 RP, reflecting their heritage of faith. Nephilim begin with 3 CP, representing their tainted bloodline. But these starting values are just the beginning. Every significant action can shift the balance:  
Gaining Righteousness Points:  
Protecting the innocent from Nephilim oppression  
Resisting the temptation to use forbidden Watcher magic  
Keeping sacred oaths and covenants  
Showing mercy to defeated enemies  
Preserving ancient knowledge of the Creator  
Gaining Corruption Points:  
Using Watcher-taught magic (even for good purposes)  
Killing surrendered enemies or non-combatants  
Breaking sacred vows or oaths  
Consorting with disembodied giant spirits (Refa'im)  
Seeking power through blood sacrifice or dark rituals  
The consequences of these choices are mechanical as well as narrative. High Corruption reduces Initiative, representing a sluggish spirit weighed down by sin. It may trigger divine judgment effects at critical moments. It opens vulnerability to demonic possession and creates social consequences with righteous NPCs. Conversely, high Righteousness grants advantages on crucial rolls, provides protection against corruption effects, and may even attract divine intervention in moments of greatest need.  
This system creates meaningful roleplaying opportunities. A Magi character might struggle with the temptation to use increasingly powerful spells, knowing each casting risks their soul. A Warrior might face the choice between executing a defeated Nephilim champion (gaining CP but ensuring they cannot return) or showing mercy (gaining RP but risking future attacks). A Priest might need to decide whether to spend precious RP to heal an ally or save it for a more critical moment.  
Survival Against Titans  
The antediluvian world is not balanced. Giants walk the earth, and humans are, by comparison, small and fragile. This is not a setting where a single human hero can easily defeat a Nephilim in single combat. Victory against titans requires cunning, preparation, teamwork, and often divine assistance.  
Nephilim Warriors stand 12 to 15 feet tall, wielding bronze greatswords that could cleave a human in half with a single stroke. They possess angelic resistance to many forms of attack and have appetites that drive them to consume entire villages when hungry. Anakim nobles wear heavy chains as symbols of status and can grapple opponents from 15 feet away. Rephaim champions radiate deathly auras that frighten mortal hearts.  
Surviving in this world requires different strategies than standard fantasy adventuring:  
Ambush and Guerrilla Tactics: Direct confrontation with Nephilim is suicide. Characters must learn to strike from hiding, use terrain to their advantage, and retreat before the giant can bring its full power to bear.  
Exploiting Weaknesses: While Nephilim are resistant to many attacks, they have vulnerabilities. Their size makes them susceptible to tripping and environmental hazards. Their arrogance often leads them to underestimate human opponents.  
Divine Assistance: The righteous line has access to miracles that can level the playing field—temporarily. But such power comes at a cost and cannot be relied upon constantly.  
Alliances: Sometimes survival means making difficult alliances—with other human tribes, with sympathetic giants (rare though they may be), or even with lesser Watchers who regret their fall.  
This theme emphasizes resource management, strategic thinking, and the importance of preparation. Characters cannot simply "level up" their way to victory against giants. They must use their wits, their environment, and whatever limited resources they can gather to overcome seemingly insurmountable odds.  
The Imminence of Judgment  
Every moment in the antediluvian world is shadowed by the knowledge that judgment is coming. This is not a distant, abstract concept but an imminent reality. The prophets have spoken. The signs are multiplying. The corruption has reached its full measure. The Flood is coming.  
This creates a unique sense of urgency and consequence. Characters know that their actions in this age will determine their fate in the age to come. There is no "reset button." No "raising from the dead" without significant cost. Death is permanent, and judgment is certain.  
For players, this means that every adventure carries weight beyond the immediate objective. Rescuing a village from Nephilim oppression isn't just about gaining treasure or experience—it's about preserving lives that might otherwise be lost before the Flood. Recovering an ancient scroll containing Enoch's prophecies isn't just about gaining knowledge—it's about preserving truth that might guide others to righteousness.  
The imminence of judgment also creates dramatic tension. Characters may receive visions or dreams of the coming Deluge, forcing them to confront their own mortality and the state of their souls. They may encounter Noah and his family, witnessing firsthand the preparations for survival. They may even be given opportunities to join the righteous line—or to reject it, embracing the corruption that surrounds them.  
Legacy and Memory  
Though the antediluvian world will be destroyed by the Flood, its legacy endures. The Refa'im survive as demons. The Watchers remain bound, awaiting final judgment. The teachings of the fallen angels persist in corrupted forms. And the memory of the age of giants lives on in myth, legend, and scattered texts.  
Characters in Nephilim Wars are not just survivors—they are preservers. They may be tasked with recording the truth of the age before it is lost. They may seek to recover artifacts or knowledge that should not be destroyed. They may work to ensure that the lessons of the antediluvian world are not forgotten, so that future generations might avoid the same mistakes.  
This theme gives players a sense of purpose beyond mere survival. Even in the face of inevitable destruction, their actions matter. The stories they tell, the records they keep, the truths they preserve—these things have value that extends beyond the Flood. They become part of a legacy that will shape the post-diluvian world.  
How to Use This Manual  
For Players  
If you are new to Pathfinder 2nd Edition, welcome\! This manual contains everything you need to create and play a character in the world of Nephilim Wars. You do not need the Pathfinder Core Rulebook to play, though it may be helpful for reference.  
Getting Started:  
Read Chapter 1 (this chapter) to understand the setting and themes  
Review Chapter 2 (Ancestries) to choose your character's lineage  
Look at Chapter 3 (Backgrounds) to determine your character's history and skills  
Examine Chapter 4 (Classes) to select your character's role and abilities  
Create your character using the rules in these chapters  
Familiarize yourself with the Soul Economy in Chapter 5
Review equipment options in Chapter 8
During Play:  
Keep track of your Righteousness Points (RP) and Corruption Points (CP) on your character sheet  
Consider how your character's choices align with their lineage and background  
Remember that this is a world of bronze-age technology and mythic scale—adapt your expectations accordingly  
Work with your Game Master to understand the campaign's specific themes and goals  
For Game Masters  
As the Game Master (GM), you are responsible for bringing the antediluvian world to life. This manual provides you with the tools to create compelling adventures, memorable NPCs, and challenging encounters in the age before the Flood.  
Preparation:  
Read through the entire manual to understand all the rules and options  
Decide on your campaign's focus (survival, rebellion, prophecy, corruption, or the Flood itself)  
Review Chapter 11 (Game Mastering) for guidance on running Nephilim Wars  
Study Chapter 12 (The Antediluvian World) to understand the setting's geography and key locations  
Prepare encounters using the creatures in Chapter 13 (Bestiary)  
Consider how the Soul Economy will impact your campaign's narrative  
During Play:  
Track each player's RP and CP, awarding points for significant actions  
Use the unique challenges of the setting (giants, forbidden knowledge, impending judgment) to create tension  
Balance encounters carefully—Nephilim are meant to be formidable opponents, not standard enemies  
Incorporate the themes of moral choice and legacy into your adventures  
Be prepared for players to make unexpected choices—this is a world where every decision matters  
For Both Players and GMs  
Nephilim Wars is designed to be a complete, standalone game. However, if you have experience with Pathfinder 2e, you will find many familiar mechanics adapted to fit the antediluvian setting. The three-action economy, proficiency system, and core rules remain largely the same, with modifications to reflect the bronze-age aesthetic and unique themes of the setting.  
Key Differences from Standard Pathfinder 2e:  
No medieval or Renaissance technology—everything is bronze-age appropriate  
No standard fantasy races (elves, dwarves, halflings)—instead, you have the ten lineages detailed in Chapter 2
The Soul Economy replaces or modifies many standard mechanics  
Magic is divided into Forbidden Sorcery (Watcher-taught) and Divine Miracles (Creator-granted)  
Giants are a constant presence and threat, not rare monsters  
The setting has a definite endpoint (the Flood), which can shape campaign structure  
Recommended Reading Order:  
Chapter 1: Introduction to the Antediluvian World (this chapter)  
Chapter 5: The Soul Economy (understanding this system is crucial)  
Chapter 2: Ancestries  
Chapter 4: Classes  
Chapter 3: Backgrounds  
Chapter 8: Equipment  
Chapter 9: Spells and Miracles  
Chapter 10: Playing the Game  
Chapter 11: Game Mastering  
Chapter 12: The Antediluvian World  
Chapter 13: Bestiary  
Chapter 14: Treasures and Artifacts
Character Creation Summary
Follow these steps to create a character for Nephilim Wars:
Step 1: Choose Your Ancestry (Chapter 2)
Select one of the ten ancestries. Record your ancestry Hit Points, Size, Speed, Ability Boosts, Ability Flaw, languages, traits, and special abilities. Choose a heritage within your ancestry.
Step 2: Choose Your Background (Chapter 3)
Select a background that fits your character concept. Record the skill training, Ability Boost, and starting equipment granted by your background.
Step 3: Choose Your Class (Chapter 4)
Select a class. Record your class Hit Points, Key Ability, Initial Proficiencies, and 1st-level class features. Your total starting Hit Points equal your ancestry HP + class HP + Constitution modifier.
Step 4: Determine Ability Scores
Start with 10 in each ability score. Apply your ancestry Ability Boosts and Ability Flaw. Apply your background Ability Boost. Apply your class Key Ability Boost. Apply four additional free Ability Boosts (each to a different score). Each boost increases a score by 2 (or by 1 if the score is already 18 or higher).
Step 5: Record Your Soul Economy Starting Values
Ancestry | Starting RP | Starting CP
Sethite | 2 | 0
Cainite | 0 | 1
Wanderer | 0 | 0
Sorcerer Clan | 0 | 2
Nephilim | 0 | 3
Rephaim | 0 | 2
Anakim | 0 | 2
Gibborim | 0 | 1
Horim | 0 | 1
Elioud | 0 | 1
Certain backgrounds and class features may modify these starting values. Your starting Net Righteousness Score equals RP minus CP.
Step 6: Select Skills
Record your trained skills from your class and background. You begin trained in the skills listed by your class, plus any granted by your background or ancestry.
Step 7: Select Feats
Choose your 1st-level ancestry feat (Chapter 2) and any class feats granted at 1st level (Chapter 4).
Step 8: Purchase Equipment
Starting Gold by Class:
Warrior: 60 gp
Gibbor: 80 gp
Hunter: 100 gp
Magi: 140 gp
Priest: 150 gp (often supplemented by community)
Artisan: 50 gp
Scribe: 45 gp
Purchase weapons, armor, and gear from Chapter 8. Add any equipment granted by your background.
Step 9: Calculate Final Statistics
AC = 10 + Dexterity modifier (up to armor's Max Dex) + armor AC bonus + proficiency bonus
Perception = Wisdom modifier + proficiency bonus
Saving Throws = ability modifier + proficiency bonus
Attack Rolls = ability modifier + proficiency bonus + item bonus
Melee Damage = ability modifier + weapon damage dice
Speed = ancestry Speed - armor Speed penalty
Step 10: Finalize Your Character
Write a brief description of your character's appearance, personality, and goals. Consider how your ancestry, background, and class inform your character's place in the antediluvian world. Discuss with your GM how your character fits into the campaign.
Tone Guide: The Bronze-Age Aesthetic  
Nephilim Wars is not a standard fantasy setting. It is specifically designed to evoke the ancient, mythic world of the antediluvian age. To maintain the proper tone and atmosphere, keep the following guidelines in mind:  
Language and Terminology  
Use Bronze-Age Appropriate Terms:  
Instead of "castle," use "fortified settlement," "city walls," or "stronghold"  
Instead of "knight," use "warrior," "champion," or "tribal fighter"  
Instead of "wizard," use "magi," "sorcerer," or "keeper of forbidden knowledge"  
Instead of "church," use "sacred grove," "altar," or "place of worship"  
Instead of "kingdom," use "tribe," "city-state," "clan," or "people"  
Avoid Anachronisms:  
No plate armor (bronze plate exists but is extremely rare and primitive)  
No steel weapons (bronze is the pinnacle of metallurgy)  
No gunpowder or firearms  
No medieval feudalism (social structures are tribal or city-based)  
No standard fantasy creatures that don't fit the biblical/apocryphal setting  
Technology Level  
The technology of the antediluvian world is sophisticated for its time but primitive by later standards:  
Metallurgy:  
Bronze is the primary metal for weapons and armor  
Iron is exceptionally rare and sacred (associated with Tubal-Cain's innovations)  
Gold and silver are used for ornamentation and ritual objects  
Copper is common for everyday items  
Weaponry:  
Swords, spears, axes, and maces made of bronze  
Bows (composite and simple) with bronze-tipped arrows  
Slings and javelins for ranged combat  
Shields made of wood reinforced with bronze  
Siege weapons are primitive—battering rams, siege towers, and catapults are in their infancy  
Armor:  
Leather and hide for light protection  
Bronze scale mail for medium protection  
Bronze plate armor exists but is heavy, expensive, and rare  
Helmets, greaves, and other pieces are worn separately rather than as full suits  
Transportation:  
Walking is the primary mode of travel  
Domesticated animals (oxen, donkeys) for carrying goods  
Chariots for elite warriors and nobility  
Ships and boats for river and coastal travel  
No riding horses (horses may exist but are not yet domesticated for riding)  
Construction:  
Mud brick and stone for buildings  
Wood for roofs and supports  
Cities have walls made of stone or brick  
Temples and important structures are larger and more ornate  
No arches or complex architectural features  
Social Structure  
Society in the antediluvian world is organized differently than medieval fantasy:  
Tribal Structure:  
Extended families form the basic social unit  
Elders hold authority based on age and wisdom  
Warriors protect the tribe and lead in battle  
Priests or shamans mediate between the people and the divine  
City-States:  
Larger settlements are ruled by chieftains or kings  
Social hierarchy includes nobles, artisans, merchants, and laborers  
Giant overlords may rule over human populations  
Slavery exists, often as a result of warfare or debt  
Nomadic Groups:  
Wanderers live in tents and move with the seasons  
They follow herds of animals or seek out wild plant foods  
They have deep knowledge of the wilderness and survival skills  
They may trade with settled communities or raid them  
Religious and Spiritual Elements  
Religion in the antediluvian world is immediate and powerful:  
The Creator:  
Known as the Most High, the Almighty, or simply "God"  
Worship involves sacrifice, prayer, and obedience to divine commands  
Prophets receive direct revelations and visions  
The righteous line maintains knowledge of the Creator's will  
The Watchers:  
Fallen angels who taught forbidden knowledge  
Some humans worship them as gods or seek their favor  
Their imprisonment does not end their influence  
Their teachings persist through secret traditions  
Spirits and Demons:  
The Refa'im (disembodied Nephilim spirits) haunt the world  
Other spirits may be benevolent or malevolent  
Possession is a real danger  
Exorcism and spiritual warfare are important practices  
Sacred Places:  
Altars built of stone for sacrifice  
Sacred groves where the divine presence is felt  
High places for observation and worship  
Burial sites that must be respected  
Moral Seriousness  
Unlike many fantasy settings where morality is flexible or abstract, Nephilim Wars treats moral choices as having real, eternal consequences:  
Righteousness Matters:  
Good actions have tangible benefits (RP)  
Evil actions have tangible costs (CP)  
Characters cannot simply "be evil" without consequences  
Redemption is possible but difficult  
Judgment is Real:  
The Flood is not a metaphor but an actual event  
Every soul will be judged  
Characters should consider the eternal impact of their choices  
The setting has a definite moral framework  
Sacrifice and Heroism:  
True heroism often involves self-sacrifice  
Standing against corruption may cost everything  
The righteous line is small but significant  
Hope exists even in the darkest times  
Scale and Scope  
The antediluvian world operates on a mythic scale:  
Giants are Truly Giant:  
Nephilim are 12–15 feet tall, not just "big humans"  
Their weapons, armor, and equipment are proportionally larger  
They consume resources at an unsustainable rate  
They view humans as inferior beings  
The World is Vast:  
Travel between settlements takes weeks or months  
Wilderness areas are truly wild and dangerous  
Cities are rare and isolated  
The world feels larger and more mysterious  
Events are Epic:  
The binding of the Watchers is a world-changing event  
The coming Flood will destroy everything  
Prophecies have real power and significance  
Individual actions can have far-reaching consequences  
Horror Elements  
While Nephilim Wars is not a horror game per se, it incorporates elements of cosmic and supernatural horror:  
Body Horror:  
The Nephilim are unnatural hybrids of angel and human  
Their size and appearance are disturbing  
Cannibalism is a real threat  
Possession transforms victims  
Cosmic Horror:  
The Watchers are fallen celestial beings of immense power  
The Flood is an act of cosmic judgment  
The scale of corruption is overwhelming  
Humanity is small and fragile in a vast, hostile universe  
Supernatural Horror:  
The Refa'im are disembodied spirits seeking bodies  
Demonic possession is a constant danger  
Forbidden knowledge corrupts those who seek it  
The dead do not always stay dead  
By maintaining these tonal elements, you will create a gaming experience that feels authentically antediluvian—ancient, mythic, morally serious, and epic in scope. The world of Nephilim Wars is not a place of comfortable fantasy but a harsh, beautiful, and terrifying age when giants walked the earth and judgment loomed on the horizon.  
Chapter 2: Ancestries (Lineages)  
Introduction to Ancestries  
In the antediluvian world, your ancestry is more than just your family tree—it is your destiny, your burden, and your place in the cosmic struggle between righteousness and corruption. The blood that flows through your veins determines not only your physical form and innate abilities but also your spiritual state in a world where divine judgment looms over all.  
Unlike standard fantasy settings where races are distinct species, the ancestries of Nephilim Wars represent different bloodlines within humanity and its giant kin. Some are blessed lineages tracing back to the righteous line of Seth. Others bear the mark of the fallen Watchers, their blood tainted by forbidden unions. Still others walk the middle path, neither fully righteous nor fully corrupted.  
This chapter presents ten complete ancestries for Nephilim Wars, each adapted to the Pathfinder 2nd Edition rules system. Whether you are a towering Nephilim titan, a righteous Sethite prophet, or a cunning Sorcerer Clan initiate, your ancestry provides the foundation upon which you will build your character.  
Human Lineages  
Sethite  
"And to Seth, to him also there was born a son; and he called his name Enos: then began men to call upon the name of the LORD." — Genesis 4:26
The Sethites are the keepers of the original faith, the righteous line that traces its ancestry back to Adam through Seth, the appointed replacement for Abel. While the world around them descends into corruption and violence, the Sethites preserve the ancient truths passed down from Eden. They are the few who still call upon the name of the Most High, maintaining the knowledge of the Creator in an age of increasing darkness.  
Sethites are typically found in isolated communities, often hidden in remote valleys or mountain regions where they can practice their faith without interference from Nephilim overlords or Cainite city-dwellers. They are scholars, prophets, and shepherds—those who value wisdom over warfare and righteousness over power. Many of the great patriarchs of the antediluvian age come from this line, including Enoch, Methuselah, and ultimately Noah.  
Physical Description: Sethites are of average human height and build, though they often possess a certain dignity and gravitas that sets them apart. Their features tend toward the refined, with thoughtful eyes and expressions that reflect their contemplative nature. They dress simply, favoring practical garments of wool and linen over the ornate clothing of Cainite cities.  
Society: Sethite communities are organized around family units and shared faith. Elders hold positions of respect based on their wisdom and spiritual insight rather than wealth or martial prowess. Their settlements are modest but well-ordered, with central gathering places for prayer and teaching. They maintain extensive oral traditions and, in some cases, written records on clay tablets or parchment.  
Relations: Sethites view the Cainites with pity and concern, seeing them as lost souls who have turned away from the Creator. They fear and oppose the Nephilim and their giant kin, recognizing them as agents of corruption. They are wary of the Sorcerer Clans, knowing that their forbidden knowledge comes at a terrible spiritual cost. Wanderers are viewed with compassion, as lost souls who may yet find their way back to righteousness.  
Sethite Ancestry
Hit Points 8 Size Medium
Speed 25 feet
Ability Boosts Two free
Ability Flaw One free  
Languages Common, plus a number of additional languages equal to your Intelligence modifier (minimum 1). Choose from Celestial, Giant, Ancient, or any other languages to which you have access (such as regional languages).  
Additional Languages You can select Celestial as a language when adding additional languages.  
Traits Human, Sethite  
Low-Light Vision You can see in dim light as though it were bright light, and in darkness as though it were dim light.  
Sethite Heritages  
At 1st level, choose one of the following heritages representing your specific lineage within the Sethite people.  
Blessed of Enoch  
Your bloodline traces directly back to Enoch, the seventh from Adam who walked with God. This heritage grants you enhanced spiritual sensitivity and divine protection.  
Blessed Heritage You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Religion checks. Additionally, you can cast Detect Evil as a divine innate spell once per day. This is a 1st-level spell.  
Shepherd of the Flock  
Your family has served as shepherds and caretakers for generations, developing a deep connection to the natural world and its creatures.  
Shepherd's Bond You gain the Animal Companion class feat, even if you don't meet the prerequisites. Your companion is a loyal sheep, goat, or other domesticated animal that has been with your family for generations. If your companion dies, you can spend a week of downtime to bond with a new one.  
Keeper of the Scrolls  
Your lineage has been entrusted with preserving ancient knowledge and sacred texts. You have been trained from childhood in the arts of reading, writing, and interpretation.  
Scroll Keeper You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Decipher Writing and Recall Knowledge. You begin play with a set of clay tablets or parchment scrolls containing ancient wisdom (worth 10 gp as trade goods, but invaluable for their knowledge).  
Prophet's Lineage  
Your family has produced prophets and visionaries who have received direct revelations from the Most High. You carry this prophetic potential within you.  
Prophetic Dreams Once per day, you can attempt a DC 15 flat check. On a success, you receive a vague but helpful vision related to an upcoming challenge or danger. The GM determines the nature and usefulness of this vision.  
Sethite Ancestry Feats  
At 1st level and every 4 levels thereafter, you gain an ancestry feat.  
1st Level Sethite Feats  
Divine Favor \[Feat 1\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Frequency Once per day  
Trigger You fail a saving throw or skill check  
You call upon the Most High for assistance. Reroll the failed saving throw or skill check. You must use the result of the second roll, even if it is worse.  
Righteous Heritage \[Feat 1\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
You begin with 2 Righteousness Points (RP) instead of the normal starting amount for your class. These points represent your blessed heritage and connection to the righteous line.  
Scriptural Knowledge \[Feat 1\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
You gain the Heal and Religion skills as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. If you are already trained in either skill, you gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks with that skill.  
Voice of Reason \[Feat 1\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
You can use your Wisdom modifier instead of your Charisma modifier for Diplomacy checks. Additionally, you gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Diplomacy checks when attempting to calm or reason with hostile creatures.  
5th Level Sethite Feats  
Blessing of Protection \[Feat 5\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Divine Favor feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Trigger An ally within 30 feet is about to take damage  
You interpose yourself spiritually between your ally and harm. The ally gains resistance 5 to all damage from the triggering attack. If the ally is a fellow Sethite, this resistance increases to 10\.  
Guided Strike \[Feat 5\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Requirements You are wielding a melee weapon  
When you Strike with a melee weapon, you can add your Wisdom modifier to the damage roll instead of your Strength or Dexterity modifier. This applies only to the first damage roll of the Strike.  
Sanctuary Aura \[Feat 5\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[A\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 minute  
You create a zone of divine protection in a 10-foot emanation centered on you. Evil creatures and corrupted beings (those with 3 or more Corruption Points) take a –1 status penalty to attack rolls while within this aura. The aura moves with you.  
Word of Warning \[Feat 5\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Frequency Once per day  
Trigger You or an ally within 60 feet is about to be surprised  
You cry out a warning that reaches the ears of your allies. The triggering creature and all allies within 60 feet of you are no longer surprised.  
9th Level Sethite Feats  
Divine Intervention \[Feat 9\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Divine Favor feat  
Frequency Once per week  
Trigger You or an ally within 30 feet is reduced to 0 Hit Points  
The Most High answers your desperate prayer. The triggering creature is restored to 1 Hit Point and gains temporary Hit Points equal to your level. Additionally, they are healed of one condition of the GM's choice.  
Righteous Fury \[Feat 9\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Requirements You have 3 or more Righteousness Points  
You can spend 3 RP as a free action when you make a Strike. The Strike deals an additional 2d6 damage of the weapon's type, and if the target is an evil creature or has 3 or more Corruption Points, the damage increases to 3d6.  
Spiritual Resilience \[Feat 9\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
You gain a \+2 status bonus to saving throws against fear effects and possession attempts. Additionally, when you succeed at a saving throw against a mental effect, you can spend 1 RP to turn the success into a critical success.  
Teaching the Righteous \[Feat 9\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Scriptural Knowledge feat  
You can spend 10 minutes instructing allies in the ways of righteousness. Up to six willing creatures gain a \+1 status bonus to saving throws against corruption effects and a \+1 status bonus to checks to resist possession for 1 hour. You can use this ability once per day.  
13th Level Sethite Feats  
Blessed Champion \[Feat 13\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Righteous Heritage feat  
Requirements You have 5 or more Righteousness Points  
Your connection to the divine is so strong that you can channel righteous power into your allies. As a single action, you can touch an ally and spend 2 RP to grant them the effects of the Bless spell for 1 minute. You can use this ability once per day.  
Divine Shield \[Feat 13\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Sanctuary Aura feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Trigger You or an ally within 30 feet is hit by an attack  
You raise a shield of divine energy. The triggering attack is treated as a miss. If the attack was made by an evil creature or a corrupted being, the attacker takes 3d6 radiant damage.  
Prophetic Insight \[Feat 13\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Prophet's Lineage heritage  
Frequency Once per day  
You receive a clear vision of an upcoming event. For the next hour, you gain a \+2 status bonus to all skill checks and saving throws. Additionally, you can reroll one failed check during this hour, using the second result even if it is worse.  
Righteous Judgment \[Feat 13\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry  
Requirements You have 5 or more Righteousness Points  
You can spend 5 RP as a single action to mark a creature within 60 feet as anathema to the righteous. For 1 minute, all attacks against the marked creature deal an additional 1d6 radiant damage, and the creature takes a –2 status penalty to saving throws against divine spells and effects. You can use this ability once per day.  
17th Level Sethite Feats  
Avatar of Righteousness \[Feat 17\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Righteous Fury feat  
Requirements You have 7 or more Righteousness Points  
Frequency Once per week  
You temporarily become an avatar of divine judgment. For 1 minute, you gain the following benefits:  
You deal an additional 3d6 radiant damage with all weapon Strikes  
You gain resistance 15 to all damage except force  
Evil creatures and corrupted beings within 30 feet of you are frightened You can spend RP to fuel any divine spell or ability as if you were a divine spellcaster of your level  
Eternal Legacy \[Feat 17\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Teaching the Righteous feat  
Your teachings and example inspire others to righteousness. When you use Teaching the Righteous, the bonuses last for 8 hours instead of 1 hour, and affected creatures gain temporary Hit Points equal to your level. Additionally, any creature that benefits from your teaching gains 1 RP at the end of the duration.  
Hand of the Creator \[Feat 17\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Divine Intervention feat  
Frequency Once per month  
Trigger A creature within 120 feet is killed by an attack or effect  
You call upon the ultimate power of the Creator to restore life. The triggering creature is restored to life with 1 Hit Point and all conditions removed. This ability cannot restore creatures that have been dead for more than 1 minute, and it cannot restore creatures killed by effects that prevent resurrection. Using this ability reduces your maximum Hit Points by 20 until you complete a full week of downtime.  
Pillar of Faith \[Feat 17\]  
Sethite  
Prerequisites Sethite ancestry, Blessed Champion feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[AA\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 minute  
You become an immovable pillar of divine power. For the duration, you gain the following benefits:  
You cannot be moved against your will  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to AC and saving throws  
Allies within 30 feet gain a \+2 status bonus to AC and saving throws  
You can spend RP to heal allies within 30 feet as a single action (spend 1 RP to heal 1d8+your level HP)  
Cainite  
"And Cain went out from the presence of the LORD, and dwelt in the land of Nod, on the east of Eden." — Genesis 4:16
The Cainites are the descendants of Cain, the first murderer, who bear the mark placed upon him by the Creator as both protection and curse. Driven from the presence of the Most High, they turned their attention to building civilization in the land of Nod. Under the guidance of Cain's descendants—particularly Jabal (father of tent-dwelling herders), Jubal (father of musicians), and Tubal-Cain (master of bronze and iron working)—the Cainites became the architects of the first cities and the pioneers of arts, crafts, and technology.  
Cainites are pragmatic, ambitious, and innovative. They believe that humanity's future lies not in waiting for divine intervention but in building, creating, and mastering the world around them. Their cities are marvels of antediluvian engineering, with bronze-reinforced walls, sophisticated irrigation systems, and bustling marketplaces. They are the metallurgists who forge the finest weapons, the musicians who fill the air with song, and the merchants who trade goods across vast distances.  
Physical Description: Cainites tend to be slightly taller and more robust than Sethites, with features that reflect their mixed heritage and urban lifestyle. They often bear visible marks of their trades—calloused hands from metalworking, ink-stained fingers from record-keeping, or the distinctive Mark of Cain itself (a faint, shimmering sigil on the forehead that only those with spiritual sight can fully perceive). Their clothing is more elaborate than that of Sethites, featuring dyed fabrics, bronze ornaments, and practical but stylish designs.  
Society: Cainite society is organized around cities and trade networks. The first great city, Enoch (named for Cain's son), serves as a cultural and economic hub. Leadership is typically held by the most skilled artisans, wealthiest merchants, or strongest warriors. Social mobility is possible through achievement and innovation. Cainites value practical knowledge, artistic expression, and material prosperity.  
Relations: Cainites view Sethites as backward and naive, clinging to outdated beliefs while the world moves forward. They fear the Nephilim but often find ways to coexist with them through tribute, trade, or service. They respect the Sorcerer Clans for their knowledge but are wary of the spiritual cost of their power. Wanderers are seen as useful trading partners but unreliable allies.  
Cainite Ancestry
Hit Points 8 Size Medium
Speed 25 feet
Ability Boosts Two free
Ability Flaw One free  
Languages Common, plus a number of additional languages equal to your Intelligence modifier (minimum 1). Choose from Giant, Ancient, Dwarven, Gnomish, or any other languages to which you have access.  
Additional Languages You can select Dwarven or Gnomish as languages when adding additional languages, reflecting your people's expertise in crafts and engineering.  
Traits Human, Cainite  
City Born You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about urban environments, crafts, trades, and city politics. You also gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Gather Information in cities and towns.  
Cainite Heritages  
At 1st level, choose one of the following heritages representing your specific lineage within the Cainite people.  
Mark of the Builder  
Your family descends from Tubal-Cain, the master craftsman who forged all kinds of tools from bronze and iron. You have inherited his skill with metal and stone.  
Builder's Heritage You gain the Crafting skill as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. Additionally, you can Craft items of up to common rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools. The GM determines which items qualify based on your specific crafting specialty (metalworking, stoneworking, woodworking, etc.).  
Mark of the Herdsman  
Your bloodline traces back to Jabal, the father of those who dwell in tents and raise livestock. You have a natural affinity for animals and the wilderness, despite your urban upbringing.  
Herdsman's Touch You gain the Nature skill as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. Additionally, you can use the Command an Animal action without having the Nature skill as trained, though you still take the normal penalty for not being trained.  
Mark of the Musician  
Your lineage descends from Jubal, the father of all who play the harp and flute. Music flows in your blood, and you can use it to influence emotions and inspire others.  
Musician's Soul You gain the Performance skill as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. Additionally, you can use Performance checks instead of Diplomacy checks to Make an Impression, and instead of Intimidation checks to Demoralize, though you still take the normal penalty for using the wrong skill.  
Mark of the Merchant  
Your family has been involved in trade and commerce for generations, building networks of contacts and developing sharp business acumen.  
Merchant's Acumen You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks to Earn Income and a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Diplomacy when dealing with merchants, traders, or anyone involved in commerce. Additionally, you can always find a buyer for mundane items at 100% of their listed value (rather than the normal 50–75%).  
Cainite Ancestry Feats  
At 1st level and every 4 levels thereafter, you gain an ancestry feat.  
1st Level Cainite Feats  
Artisan's Tools \[Feat 1\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry  
You gain proficiency with artisan's tools (your choice of alchemist's, armorer's, blacksmith's, carpenter's, leatherworker's, or mason's tools). Additionally, you can use these tools to Aid yourself or others on skill checks related to your chosen craft.  
City Knowledge \[Feat 1\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry  
You gain the Society skill as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. If you are already trained in Society, you gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Society checks. Additionally, you can use Society checks to Recall Knowledge about urban environments, city politics, and trade networks.  
Mark of Cain \[Feat 1\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry  
Frequency Once per day  
Trigger A creature reduces you to 0 Hit Points  
The ancient mark upon your forehead flares with divine power. The triggering creature takes 1d6 divine damage and must attempt a DC 15 Will save. On a failure, the creature is frightened 1\. This ability represents the protection granted to Cain by the Creator: "Therefore whosoever slayeth Cain, vengeance shall be taken on him sevenfold" (Genesis 4:15).  
Urban Survivor \[Feat 1\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry  
You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Stealth checks in urban environments and a \+1 circumstance bonus to Perception checks to notice hidden dangers or ambushes in cities and towns. Additionally, you can use the Subsist action in urban environments without taking the normal penalty for not being trained in Society.  
5th Level Cainite Feats  
Advanced Crafting \[Feat 5\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Artisan's Tools feat  
You can Craft items of up to uncommon rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools. Additionally, the time required to Craft items is reduced by 25%.  
City Connections \[Feat 5\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, City Knowledge feat  
Frequency Once per day  
You can use your urban connections to gain assistance or information. This functions as the Request downtime activity, but it takes only 10 minutes instead of 1 hour, and you automatically succeed at the DC 15 Diplomacy check. The GM determines what kind of assistance or information is available based on your location and the nature of your request.  
Improved Mark of Cain \[Feat 5\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Mark of Cain feat  
The damage from your Mark of Cain ability increases to 2d6 divine damage, and the DC of the Will save increases to 18\. Additionally, on a critical failure, the triggering creature is frightened 2 instead of frightened 1\.  
Trade Network \[Feat 5\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Mark of the Merchant heritage  
Frequency Once per week  
You can call upon your trade network to acquire items or services that would normally be unavailable. This functions as the Purchase downtime activity, but you can purchase items of up to 500 gp in value that are not normally available in your current settlement. The GM determines what items or services are available through your network.  
9th Level Cainite Feats  
Master Artisan \[Feat 9\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Advanced Crafting feat  
You can Craft items of up to rare rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools. Additionally, items you Craft have their Hardness increased by 2 and their Hit Points increased by 10\.  
City Lord \[Feat 9\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, City Connections feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[A\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 minute  
You command the respect and authority of the city. For the duration, you gain a \+2 status bonus to Diplomacy and Intimidation checks, and creatures with the humanoid trait that are not your enemies treat you as friendly. This ability does not work on creatures that are already hostile or on creatures that are not influenced by social status.  
Mark of Divine Protection \[Feat 9\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Improved Mark of Cain feat  
The damage from your Mark of Cain ability increases to 3d6 divine damage, and the DC of the Will save increases to 21\. Additionally, when the ability triggers, you are restored to 1 Hit Point and gain temporary Hit Points equal to your level.  
Urban Warfare \[Feat 9\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry  
You excel at combat in urban environments. You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to attack rolls when fighting in cities, towns, or other urban environments. Additionally, you can use the High Ground action without needing to be on higher elevation, provided you are fighting in an urban environment with buildings, walls, or other structures that provide tactical advantages.  
13th Level Cainite Feats  
Legendary Crafter \[Feat 13\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Master Artisan feat  
You can Craft items of up to legendary rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools. Additionally, you can imbue items you Craft with special properties. Once per day, you can spend 1 hour crafting to add one of the following properties to a weapon, armor, or shield you are crafting:  
The item deals an additional 1d6 damage of a type appropriate to the item (chosen when you add the property)  
The item grants a \+1 item bonus to a specific skill check (chosen when you add the property)  
The item has the Returning property (if it is a thrown weapon)  
Metropolitan Influence \[Feat 13\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, City Lord feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[AA\] (concentrate)  
Duration 10 minutes  
Your influence extends throughout the city. For the duration, you can use the City Lord ability as a free action, and its effects extend to all allies within 60 feet of you. Additionally, you can use the City Connections ability twice per day instead of once per day.  
Seal of Cain \[Feat 13\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Mark of Divine Protection feat  
Frequency Once per week  
Trigger You are reduced to 0 Hit Points  
The mark of Cain flares with such intensity that it not only protects you but also punishes your attacker. The triggering creature takes 5d6 divine damage and must attempt a DC 24 Will save. On a failure, the creature is frightened 3 and cannot attack you or your allies for 1 minute. On a critical failure, the creature is also stunned for 1 round. You are restored to half your maximum Hit Points and gain temporary Hit Points equal to your level.  
Urban Master \[Feat 13\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Urban Warfare feat  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to attack rolls when fighting in urban environments. Additionally, you can use the Cover action without needing to be behind physical cover, provided you are in an urban environment with buildings, walls, or other structures. You also gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Stealth checks in urban environments.  
17th Level Cainite Feats  
Architect of Civilization \[Feat 17\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Legendary Crafter feat  
Frequency Once per month  
Action \[AAA\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 hour  
You channel the creative spirit of your ancestors to build or repair structures at incredible speed. You can construct a building or fortification of up to 1,000 square feet in area, or repair damage to existing structures of up to 5,000 square feet in area. The structures you create are of masterwork quality and have Hardness and Hit Points appropriate for their materials. This ability can also be used to create temporary fortifications, bridges, or other structures that last for the duration.  
Cainite Dynasty \[Feat 17\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Metropolitan Influence feat  
Frequency Once per week  
Action \[AAA\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 day  
You establish your family's dominance over a city or settlement. For the duration, you and your allies gain the following benefits while in the affected settlement:  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to Diplomacy and Intimidation checks  
You can use the City Connections ability three times per day instead of once per day  
Merchants and tradespeople offer you a 50% discount on all purchases  
The settlement's guards and officials treat you and your allies as honored guests  
Mark of the First City \[Feat 17\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Seal of Cain feat  
Frequency Once per month  
Trigger You or an ally within 30 feet is reduced to 0 Hit Points  
The mark of Cain expands to protect your allies as well as yourself. The triggering creature takes 8d6 divine damage and must attempt a DC 28 Will save. On a failure, the creature is frightened 4, stunned for 1 round, and cannot attack you or your allies for 1 minute. On a critical failure, the creature is also banished to a pocket dimension for 1 minute (Will save ends). You and all allies within 30 feet of you are restored to half your maximum Hit Points and gain temporary Hit Points equal to your level.  
Master of the Urban Labyrinth \[Feat 17\]  
Cainite  
Prerequisites Cainite ancestry, Urban Master feat  
You gain a \+4 circumstance bonus to attack rolls when fighting in urban environments. Additionally, you can use the Cover action as a free action, and you gain a \+4 circumstance bonus to Stealth checks in urban environments. You can also use the Subsist action in urban environments to find food, shelter, and other necessities for yourself and up to five allies without taking any penalty.  
Wanderer  
"And the sons of God saw the daughters of men that they were fair; and they took them wives of all which they chose." — Genesis 6:The Wanderers are those who rejected both the righteous path of Seth and the city-building ways of Cain. They chose instead to live as nomads, following the seasons and the herds across the vast wilderness of the antediluvian world. Some Wanderers are descendants of early humans who never settled in cities. Others are refugees from Nephilim oppression or Cainite corruption, seeking freedom in the untamed lands. Still others are seekers of truth, believing that answers lie not in cities or temples but in the wild places where the Creator's presence can still be felt.  
Wanderers are self-reliant, adaptable, and deeply connected to the natural world. They live in tents made of animal hides, travel with their herds of sheep and goats, and survive through hunting, gathering, and trade with settled communities. They are skilled trackers, expert survivalists, and fierce warriors when provoked. Their knowledge of the wilderness is unmatched, and they often serve as guides for those brave enough to venture into the untamed lands.  
Physical Description: Wanderers tend to be lean and weathered, with sun-darkened skin and wind-tousled hair. Their bodies bear the marks of a hard life—scars from battles with wild beasts, calloused hands from handling tools and weapons, and the lean muscle of those who travel great distances on foot. They dress in practical clothing made of leather, wool, and fur, often decorated with beads, feathers, or other tokens collected during their travels.  
Society: Wanderer society is organized around extended family groups or tribes, each led by elders chosen for their wisdom, experience, and ability to provide for the group. Leadership is informal and based on respect rather than formal authority. Decisions are made through consensus, with elders offering guidance but not commands. Wanderers value freedom, self-reliance, and harmony with nature.  
Relations: Wanderers view Sethites with respect but see their settled lifestyle as limiting. They have a complex relationship with Cainites—trading with them for goods they cannot produce themselves, but wary of their urban ways and technological innovations. They fear the Nephilim but have learned to avoid them through stealth and knowledge of the wilderness. They are suspicious of the Sorcerer Clans, seeing their forbidden knowledge as a corruption of the natural order.  
Wanderer Ancestry
Hit Points 10 Size Medium
Speed 30 feet
Ability Boosts Two free
Ability Flaw One free  
Languages Common, plus a number of additional languages equal to your Intelligence modifier (minimum 1). Choose from Giant, Sylvan, Druidic, or any other languages to which you have access.  
Additional Languages You can select Sylvan or Druidic as languages when adding additional languages, reflecting your people's connection to the natural world.  
Traits Human, Wanderer  
Survivalist You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Subsist, Survival, and to Recall Knowledge about wilderness environments, plants, animals, and weather patterns.  
Wanderer Heritages  
At 1st level, choose one of the following heritages representing your specific lineage within the Wanderer people.  
Child of the Steppe  
Your family has roamed the open grasslands for generations, developing an intimate knowledge of the plains and their creatures.  
Steppe Runner Your base Speed increases to 35 feet. Additionally, you gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Acrobatics checks to Tumble Through and Athletics checks to High Jump or Long Jump.  
Mountain Guide  
Your bloodline traces back to those who first ventured into the high mountains, learning to navigate treacherous passes and survive in thin air.  
Mountain Climber You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Athletics checks to Climb. Additionally, you are not hampered by difficult terrain caused by rocks, scree, or steep slopes, and you can move through such terrain at full Speed.  
Desert Nomad  
Your ancestors learned to survive in the harshest deserts, finding water where none seemed to exist and navigating by the stars.  
Desert Hardy You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Fortitude saves against environmental effects caused by extreme heat or dehydration. Additionally, you need only half the normal amount of water to avoid the effects of dehydration.  
Forest Stalker  
Your family has lived in the deep forests since time immemorial, moving silently among the trees and understanding the language of the wild.  
Forest Stealth You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Stealth checks in forest environments. Additionally, you can use the Hide action even when you are only partially concealed by foliage, and you can Sneak without needing cover or concealment in forest environments.  
Wanderer Ancestry Feats  
At 1st level and every 4 levels thereafter, you gain an ancestry feat.  
1st Level Wanderer Feats  
Natural Tracker \[Feat 1\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry  
You gain the Survival skill as trained, even if you don't meet the normal prerequisites. If you are already trained in Survival, you gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Survival checks. Additionally, you can Track creatures without needing to succeed at a Survival check, though you still take a –5 penalty if you are not trained in Survival.  
Swift Footed \[Feat 1\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry  
Your base Speed increases by 5 feet. This increase is cumulative with other effects that increase your Speed.  
Wilderness Lore \[Feat 1\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry  
You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about plants, animals, weather patterns, and wilderness environments. Additionally, you can use the Subsist action in wilderness environments without taking the normal penalty for not being trained in Survival.  
Wanderer's Endurance \[Feat 1\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry  
You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Fortitude saves against environmental effects (extreme cold, extreme heat, storms, etc.). Additionally, you can go without food or water for one additional day before suffering the effects of starvation or dehydration.  
5th Level Wanderer Feats  
Desert Survival \[Feat 5\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Desert Nomad heritage  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Fortitude saves against environmental effects caused by extreme heat or dehydration. Additionally, you need only one-quarter the normal amount of water to avoid the effects of dehydration, and you can go without water for twice as long as normal before suffering the effects of dehydration.  
Mountain Master \[Feat 5\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Mountain Guide heritage  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Athletics checks to Climb. Additionally, you are not hampered by difficult terrain caused by rocks, scree, steep slopes, or ice, and you can move through such terrain at full Speed. You also gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Acrobatics checks to Balance on narrow or unstable surfaces.  
Pathfinder \[Feat 5\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Natural Tracker feat  
Frequency Once per day  
You can find a safe path through even the most treacherous wilderness. This functions as the Guidance spell, but it applies to Survival checks to Navigate and to avoid environmental hazards. The bonus lasts for 1 hour.  
Steppe Strider \[Feat 5\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Child of the Steppe heritage  
Your base Speed increases by an additional 5 feet (for a total increase of 10 feet from your base Speed). Additionally, you gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Acrobatics checks to Tumble Through and Athletics checks to High Jump or Long Jump.  
9th Level Wanderer Feats  
Forest Ghost \[Feat 9\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Forest Stalker heritage  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Stealth checks in forest environments. Additionally, you can use the Hide action even when you are observed, provided you are in a forest environment with sufficient foliage for concealment. You can also Sneak without needing cover or concealment in forest environments.  
Master Tracker \[Feat 9\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Pathfinder feat  
You can Track creatures with a \+2 circumstance bonus to your Survival checks. Additionally, you can follow tracks that are up to 24 hours old without penalty, and tracks that are up to 48 hours old with only a –2 penalty (instead of the normal –5 penalty).  
Survival Mastery \[Feat 9\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Wanderer's Endurance feat  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Fortitude saves against environmental effects. Additionally, you can go without food or water for twice as long as normal before suffering the effects of starvation or dehydration. You also gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks to Subsist in wilderness environments.  
Wanderer's Wisdom \[Feat 9\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry  
You have learned to read the signs of the wilderness. You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Perception checks to notice hidden dangers, ambushes, or unusual phenomena in wilderness environments. Additionally, you can use Survival checks instead of Perception checks to Sense Motive when dealing with creatures that have the animal trait.  
13th Level Wanderer Feats  
Desert Lord \[Feat 13\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Desert Survival feat  
You gain immunity to environmental effects caused by extreme heat or dehydration. Additionally, you need no water to survive, and you can go without food indefinitely (though you still need to eat to regain Hit Points through natural healing). You can also create water from desert air once per day, creating enough water to sustain yourself and up to five allies for 24 hours.  
Mountain Lord \[Feat 13\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Mountain Master feat  
You gain a \+4 circumstance bonus to Athletics checks to Climb. Additionally, you can climb vertical surfaces (including sheer cliffs and ice walls) without needing to make Athletics checks. You are not hampered by difficult terrain of any kind caused by natural features (rocks, scree, slopes, ice, snow, etc.), and you can move through such terrain at double Speed. You also gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to Acrobatics checks to Balance on narrow or unstable surfaces.  
Uncanny Tracker \[Feat 13\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Master Tracker feat  
You can Track creatures with a \+4 circumstance bonus to your Survival checks. Additionally, you can follow tracks that are up to 72 hours old without penalty, and tracks that are up to 1 week old with only a –2 penalty. You can also track creatures across surfaces that normally leave no tracks (water, stone, etc.) with a –5 penalty instead of the normal impossibility.  
Wanderer's Legacy \[Feat 13\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Wanderer's Wisdom feat  
Your connection to the wilderness is so deep that it grants you supernatural abilities. You gain low-light vision if you did not already have it, and you can use the following innate spell once per day: Speak with Plants. Additionally, animals are naturally friendly toward you, and you can use Diplomacy checks to Make an Impression on creatures with the animal trait without taking the normal penalty for not sharing a language.  
17th Level Wanderer Feats  
Child of the Wild \[Feat 17\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Wanderer's Legacy feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[A\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 minute  
You become one with the wilderness. For the duration, you gain the following benefits:  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to Stealth checks in wilderness environments  
You can move through natural difficult terrain at double Speed  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to Survival checks  
You can communicate telepathically with animals and plants within 60 feet  
You gain resistance 10 to damage from natural environmental effects (extreme cold, extreme heat, storms, etc.)  
Desert God \[Feat 17\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Desert Lord feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[A\] (concentrate)  
Duration 10 minutes  
You command the power of the desert. For the duration, you gain the following benefits:  
You are immune to all environmental effects  
You can create water at will, creating enough to sustain any number of creatures  
You can summon a sandstorm in a 60-foot burst centered on a point you can see within 120 feet. The sandstorm lasts for 1 minute and obscures vision (creatures more than 10 feet away have the concealed condition). Creatures caught in the sandstorm take 4d6 bludgeoning damage (Reflex save for half) at the start of their turn.  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to Fortitude saves  
Mountain God \[Feat 17\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Mountain Lord feat  
Frequency Once per day  
Action \[A\] (concentrate)  
Duration 10 minutes  
You command the power of the mountains. For the duration, you gain the following benefits:  
You can move through solid rock and earth as if it were air (but not metal or other non-natural materials)  
You can cause earthquakes in a 120-foot burst centered on a point you can see within 240 feet. The earthquake lasts for 1 minute and creates difficult terrain. Structures in the area must attempt a DC 25 Fortitude save or collapse.  
You gain resistance 15 to bludgeoning, piercing, and slashing damage from non-magical attacks  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to Reflex saves  
Spirit of the Wilderness \[Feat 17\]  
Wanderer  
Prerequisites Wanderer ancestry, Uncanny Tracker feat  
Frequency Once per week  
Action \[AAA\] (concentrate)  
Duration 1 hour  
You become a living embodiment of the wilderness. For the duration, you gain the following benefits:  
You gain a \+6 status bonus to all skill checks related to wilderness environments (Survival, Nature, Stealth in wilderness, etc.)  
You can move through any terrain (natural or artificial) at double Speed  
You can communicate telepathically with any creature that has the animal or plant trait within 120 feet  
You can summon 1d4+2 animals of up to 10th level to aid you. These animals appear in unoccupied spaces within 60 feet of you and follow your mental commands. They remain for the duration or until slain.  
You gain immunity to all environmental effects and a \+4 status bonus to all saving throws  
Sorcerer Clan  
"And the angels, the children of the heaven, saw and lusted after them, and they said to one another: 'Come, let us choose us wives from among the children of men and beget us children.'" — 1 Enoch 6:The Sorcerer Clans are humans who have been initiated into the forbidden mysteries taught by the Watchers. Unlike the Nephilim and their giant kin, who are the physical offspring of Watchers and humans, the Sorcerer Clans are purely human—but they bear the spiritual mark of their association with fallen angels. They are the keepers of Azazel's arts of warfare, Semyaza's enchantments, Baraqel's astrology, and all the other forbidden knowledge that the Watchers imparted to humanity.  
Sorcerer Clans operate in secret, hidden within Cainite cities or dwelling in isolated enclaves where they can practice their arts without interference from righteous Sethites or oppressive Nephilim. They are scholars, alchemists, astrologers, and ritualists—those who seek power through knowledge rather than faith or strength. Their abilities are feared and respected throughout the antediluvian world, for they can accomplish feats that seem miraculous to ordinary humans.  
Physical Description: Sorcerer Clan members often bear visible signs of their initiation—the Watcher's Mark, a mystical brand that appears on their skin, typically on the forehead, hands, or chest. This mark glows faintly when they use their powers and may change appearance based on which Watcher's teachings they follow most closely. Their eyes sometimes have an unnatural quality—pupils that are too large, irises that shift color, or a faint luminescence in darkness. They dress in robes or garments that conceal their marks when necessary, but often display them proudly among their own kind.  
Society: Sorcerer Clans are organized into secretive brotherhoods or sisterhoods, each dedicated to the teachings of a specific Watcher or group of Watchers. Leadership is based on knowledge and power rather than birth or wealth. Apprenticeship is the primary means of advancement, with masters passing down their secrets to chosen students. Sorcerer Clans maintain extensive libraries of forbidden texts, star charts, alchemical formulas, and ritual diagrams.  
Relations: Sorcerer Clans view Sethites as naive fools who reject power in favor of empty faith. They respect Cainites for their practical knowledge but see them as limited by their material focus. They have a complex relationship with the Nephilim—sometimes serving them as advisors or weaponsmiths, sometimes opposing them as rivals for power. Wanderers are generally avoided, as their connection to the natural world makes them resistant to many sorcerous influences.  
Sorcerer Clan Ancestry
Hit Points 6 Size Medium
Speed 25 feet
Ability Boosts Two free
Ability Flaw One free  
Languages Common, plus a number of additional languages equal to your Intelligence modifier (minimum 1). Choose from Celestial, Abyssal, Ancient, or any other languages to which you have access.  
Additional Languages You can select Celestial or Abyssal as languages when adding additional languages, reflecting your people's connection to the Watchers and their forbidden knowledge.  
Traits Human, Sorcerer Clan  
Dark Insight You can cast the Detect Magic spell as an occult innate spell once per day. This is a 1st-level spell.  
Sorcerer Clan Heritages  
At 1st level, choose one of the following heritages representing your specific lineage within the Sorcerer Clan people.  
Azazel's Disciple  
Your bloodline has been initiated into the teachings of Azazel, the Watcher who revealed the arts of warfare, metallurgy, and vanity to humanity.  
Warrior's Knowledge You gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about weapons, armor, and combat techniques. Additionally, you can Craft weapons and armor of up to common rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools.  
Semyaza's Acolyte  
Your family descends from those who first learned the enchantments and root-cutting arts taught by Semyaza, the leader of the 200 Watchers.
