Class Feats  
Levels 1-5:  
Blessing of Protection \[Feat 1\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
Once per day, as a single action, you can touch an ally and grant them resistance 5 to all damage for 1 minute. This represents your ability to channel divine protection to those in need. If the ally is a fellow Sethite or has 5 or more RP, this resistance increases to 10\.  
Healing Touch \[Feat 1\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Wisdom 14+  
You can heal others through divine power. As a single action, you can touch a willing creature and restore 1d8+your Wisdom modifier Hit Points. You can use this ability a number of times per day equal to your Wisdom modifier (minimum 1). This represents the most basic form of divine healing available to Priests.  
Righteous Heritage \[Feat 1\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
You begin with 2 additional Righteousness Points (RP) beyond what your ancestry provides. These points represent your blessed heritage and deep connection to the righteous line. This feat is particularly valuable for Priests who do not come from Sethite backgrounds.  
Word of Warning \[Feat 1\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
Once per day, when you or an ally within 60 feet is about to be surprised, you can cry out a divine warning. The triggering creature and all allies within 60 feet are no longer surprised. This represents your spiritual sensitivity to danger and your ability to alert others through divine insight.  
Levels 6-10:  
Blessed Champion \[Feat 6\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Blessing of Protection feat  
Once per day, as a single action, you can touch an ally and grant them the effects of the Bless spell for 1 minute. The ally gains a \+1 status bonus to attack rolls, saving throws, and skill checks. If the ally has 5 or more RP, this bonus increases to \+2.  
Divine Healing \[Feat 6\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Healing Touch feat  
The healing from your Healing Touch ability increases to 2d8+your Wisdom modifier Hit Points. Additionally, you can spend 1 RP when using Healing Touch to also remove one condition of the GM's choice from the target (such as sickened, fatigued, or frightened).  
Guided Strike \[Feat 8\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
When you make a melee Strike with a weapon, you can add your Wisdom modifier to the damage roll instead of your Strength or Dexterity modifier. This applies only to the first damage roll of the Strike. This represents your ability to channel divine power through your weapon attacks.  
Teaching the Righteous \[Feat 10\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Righteous Heritage feat  
Once per day, as a 10-minute activity, you can instruct allies in the ways of righteousness. Up to six willing creatures gain a \+1 status bonus to saving throws against corruption effects and a \+1 status bonus to checks to resist possession for 1 hour. Additionally, each creature that benefits from your teaching gains 1 RP at the end of the duration.  
Levels 11-15:  
Blessed Weapon \[Feat 12\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Guided Strike feat  
Once per day, as a single action, you can touch a weapon and bless it with divine power. For 1 minute, the weapon deals an additional 1d6 radiant damage on each Strike, and attacks with the weapon are treated as magical for the purpose of overcoming resistances. If the weapon is used against a corrupted being (3+ CP) or an undead creature, the additional damage increases to 2d6.  
Divine Intervention \[Feat 12\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Divine Intervention feature  
You can use your Divine Intervention ability twice per week instead of once per week. Additionally, when you use Divine Intervention, you can choose to restore all allies within 30 feet to half their maximum Hit Points instead of restoring one ally to full Hit Points.  
Righteous Fury \[Feat 14\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
Requirements You have 3 or more RP  
You can spend 3 RP as a free action when you make a Strike. The Strike deals an additional 2d6 damage of the weapon's type, and if the target is an evil creature or has 3 or more CP, the damage increases to 3d6. This represents your ability to channel righteous anger into your attacks.  
Spiritual Resilience \[Feat 14\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class  
You gain a \+2 status bonus to saving throws against fear effects and possession attempts. Additionally, when you succeed at a saving throw against a mental effect, you can spend 1 RP to turn the success into a critical success. This represents your strong spiritual defenses against corruption and demonic influence.  
Levels 16-20:  
Avatar of Judgment \[Feat 18\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Avatar of Righteousness feature  
When you use your Avatar of Righteousness ability, the duration increases to 10 minutes instead of 1 minute. Additionally, while in this form, you can spend RP to fuel any divine spell or ability as if you were a divine spellcaster of your level, and you gain the ability to fly at a Speed of 60 feet.  
Eternal Legacy \[Feat 18\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Teaching the Righteous feat  
When you use Teaching the Righteous, the bonuses last for 8 hours instead of 1 hour, and affected creatures gain temporary Hit Points equal to your level. Additionally, any creature that benefits from your teaching gains 2 RP at the end of the duration instead of 1 RP. This represents your ability to inspire lasting righteousness in others.  
Hand of the Creator \[Feat 20\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Divine Intervention feat  
Once per month, when a creature within 120 feet is killed by an attack or effect, you can call upon the ultimate power of the Creator to restore life. The triggering creature is restored to life with 1 Hit Point and all conditions removed. This ability cannot restore creatures that have been dead for more than 1 minute, and it cannot restore creatures killed by effects that prevent resurrection. Using this ability reduces your maximum Hit Points by 20 until you complete a full week of downtime.  
Pillar of Faith \[Feat 20\]  
Priest  
Prerequisites Priest class, Blessed Champion feat  
Once per day, as a two-action activity, you become an immovable pillar of divine power. For 1 minute, you gain the following benefits:  
You cannot be moved against your will  
You gain a \+4 status bonus to AC and saving throws  
Allies within 30 feet gain a \+2 status bonus to AC and saving throws  
You can spend RP to heal allies within 30 feet as a single action (spend 1 RP to heal 1d8+your level HP)  
You can use your Turn Unholy ability without expending its daily use  
This represents your ability to become a focal point of divine protection and power for your allies.  
Example Build  
Enoch, Sethite Prophet — A Sethite Priest who has dedicated his life to preserving the righteous line and warning others of the coming judgment. With high Wisdom and Charisma, he channels divine miracles to heal the wounded, protect the faithful, and turn back corrupted beings. His Righteous Aura provides constant protection to his allies, while his Turn Unholy ability keeps spirits and corrupted creatures at bay. Enoch begins with 4 RP (2 from his Sethite heritage and 2 from the Righteous Heritage feat), which he carefully manages to fuel his most powerful miracles. His goal is to spread the message of righteousness throughout the antediluvian world, preparing as many souls as possible for the coming Flood.  
Artisan  
The Artisan stands as the master of Tubal-Cain's legacy, a practitioner of the metallurgical and mechanical arts that define antediluvian civilization. Unlike the divine miracles of the Priest or the forbidden magic of the Magi, the Artisan's power comes from human ingenuity, craftsmanship, and the practical application of knowledge. They are the bronze-smiths who forge weapons capable of piercing giant hide, the engineers who design fortifications that can withstand Nephilim assaults, and the inventors who create tools and devices that allow mortals to challenge titanic foes.  
Artisans trace their lineage back to Tubal-Cain, the master craftsman mentioned in Genesis 4:22 who "forged all kinds of tools out of bronze and iron." They are the inheritors of his knowledge and skill, preserving and expanding upon the metallurgical arts that he pioneered. Some Artisans come from Cainite families who have maintained these traditions for generations, passing down secret techniques and formulas from parent to child. Others are self-taught innovators who have discovered the principles of metalworking through experimentation and observation. A few are even Sethites or Wanderers who have learned these arts out of necessity, recognizing that practical skills are as important as spiritual faith in a dangerous world.  
In society, Artisans occupy a position of practical importance and economic influence. Their skills are in constant demand, whether from tribal communities needing weapons and tools, from Nephilim warlords requiring arms and armor for their armies, or from Cainite cities building fortifications and infrastructure. Artisans often form guilds or brotherhoods to protect their knowledge and maintain standards of quality. These organizations can wield significant political power, controlling the flow of weapons and tools that determine the balance of power between different groups.  
The Artisan's approach to their craft reflects the bronze-age setting and the practical needs of survival in a world dominated by giants. They do not create abstract works of art for aesthetic appreciation but functional items that serve specific purposes—weapons that can kill Nephilim, armor that can protect against titanic blows, traps that can capture or kill giant raiders, and tools that make survival in the wilderness possible. Their work requires precision, patience, and deep understanding of materials and their properties. They know which alloys are strongest, which tempering techniques produce the sharpest edges, and which designs are most effective for specific tasks.  
The central feature of the Artisan class is their ability to create and use mechanical devices and traps. Unlike spellcasters who rely on supernatural power, Artisans use their knowledge of engineering and mechanics to create practical solutions to problems. They can build siege engines to breach giant fortifications, construct hidden traps to ambush Nephilim patrols, or design specialized weapons that exploit the weaknesses of titanic foes. This practical approach makes them invaluable members of any adventuring party, providing tools and devices that can overcome challenges that would defeat purely martial or magical approaches.  
Class Statistics  
Key Ability: Intelligence or Strength  
Hit Points: 8 plus your Constitution modifier  
Initial Proficiencies:  
Perception: Trained  
Saving Throws: Fortitude Trained, Reflex Expert, Will Trained  
Skills: Trained in Crafting and four other skills of your choice  
Attacks: Trained in simple weapons, martial weapons, and unarmed attacks  
Defenses: Trained in light armor and medium armor  
Class DC: Trained in Crafting DC  
Class Features  
Levels 1-5: At 1st level, Artisans gain Master Craftsman, allowing them to Craft items of up to common rarity without needing a formula, provided they have the appropriate raw materials and tools. They also gain the Crafting skill as trained if they don't already have it. At 2nd level, Bronze Working grants them expertise in metallurgy. They gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Craft metal items, and they can identify the properties and quality of metal items with a successful check. At 3rd level, Trap Maker allows them to create mechanical traps. As a 10-minute activity, they can create a simple trap that affects a 10-foot square area. Creatures entering the trapped area must attempt a Reflex save (DC \= 10 \+ your level \+ your Intelligence modifier) or take 1d6 damage of a type appropriate to the trap (piercing from sharpened stakes, bludgeoning from falling rocks, etc.). At 4th level, Structural Weakness grants them the ability to identify weak points in constructions. They gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about structures, fortifications, and mechanical devices. At 5th level, Extra Attack enables two attacks when using the Attack action.  
Levels 6-10: At 6th level, Artisans gain Advanced Crafting, allowing them to Craft items of up to uncommon rarity without needing a formula. The time required to Craft items is reduced by 25%. At 7th level, Improved Trap Maker enhances their trap-making ability. The damage from their traps increases to 2d6, and they can create traps that have additional effects (such as restraining creatures or creating difficult terrain). They can have up to three active traps at a time. At 8th level, Mechanical Insight grants them a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks to Disable Devices and to checks to Recall Knowledge about mechanical devices and traps. At 9th level, Combat Mastery allows them to make an additional attack when using the Attack action, though this third attack takes a \-5 penalty. At 10th level, Master of Metallurgy allows them to Craft metal items of up to rare rarity without needing a formula. Items they Craft have their Hardness increased by 2 and their Hit Points increased by 10\.  
Levels 11-15: At 11th level, Artisans gain Legendary Crafter, allowing them to Craft items of up to legendary rarity without needing a formula. At 12th level, Deadly Traps increases the damage from their traps to 3d6, and they can create traps that have multiple effects or trigger multiple times. They can have up to five active traps at a time. At 13th level, Perfect Structural Weakness grants them a \+3 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about structures and a \+2 circumstance bonus to attack rolls against objects and structures. At 14th level, Mechanical Mastery allows them to create complex mechanical devices such as siege engines, clockwork automatons, or advanced traps with multiple triggers and effects. At 15th level, Artisan's Ingenuity allows them to improvise tools and devices from available materials. Once per day, they can create a temporary tool or device that grants them a \+2 item bonus to a specific skill check or attack roll for 1 hour.  
Levels 16-20: At 16th level, Artisans gain Master Artisan, allowing them to imbue items they Craft with special properties. Once per day, they can spend 1 hour crafting to add one of the following properties to a weapon, armor, or shield they are crafting:  
The item deals an additional 1d6 damage of a type appropriate to the item (chosen when you add the property)  
The item grants a \+1 item bonus to a specific skill check (chosen when you add the property)  
The item has the Returning property (if it is a thrown weapon)  
At 17th level, Supreme Trap Maker increases the damage from their traps to 4d6, and they can create traps that affect areas up to 20 feet in diameter. They can have up to seven active traps at a time. At 18th level, Architect of Civilization allows them to construct buildings or fortifications of up to 1,000 square feet in area, or repair damage to existing structures of up to 5,000 square feet in area, in just 1 hour. The structures they create are of masterwork quality and have Hardness and Hit Points appropriate for their materials. At 19th level, Martial Mastery allows them to make four attacks when using the Attack action, with the fourth attack taking a \-10 penalty. At 20th level, Legendary Artisan grants them the ability to create artifacts—items of immense power that can change the course of history. Once per month, they can spend a week of downtime creating an artifact with properties determined by the GM.

Class Feats  
Levels 1-5:  
Artisan's Tools \[Feat 1\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class  
You gain proficiency with artisan's tools (your choice of alchemist's, armorer's, blacksmith's, carpenter's, leatherworker's, or mason's tools). Additionally, you can use these tools to Aid yourself or others on skill checks related to your chosen craft. This represents your specialized training in a particular craft.  
Quick Repair \[Feat 1\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class  
You can repair damaged items quickly and efficiently. As a single action, you can restore an item to full Hit Points, provided it has the broken condition and is of common rarity or lower. For items of uncommon rarity or higher, you can restore 10 Hit Points to the item as a single action. This represents your ability to make emergency repairs in the field.  
Siege Training \[Feat 1\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class  
You gain proficiency with siege weapons. Additionally, you can Craft siege weapons of up to common rarity without needing a formula, provided you have the appropriate raw materials and tools. This represents your knowledge of large-scale weaponry designed to breach fortifications or defeat giant foes.  
Weapon Smithing \[Feat 4\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Bronze Working feature  
You can Craft weapons of up to uncommon rarity without needing a formula. Additionally, weapons you Craft have their critical threat range increased by 1 (for example, a weapon with a ×2 critical multiplier gains a 19-20 critical threat range). This represents your expertise in creating deadly weapons.  
Levels 6-10:  
Advanced Artisan's Tools \[Feat 6\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Artisan's Tools feat  
You gain proficiency with a second type of artisan's tools. Additionally, when you use your artisan's tools to Aid yourself or others, the bonus increases to \+3 instead of \+2. This represents your broad expertise across multiple crafts.  
Fortification Building \[Feat 6\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Structural Weakness feature  
You can construct simple fortifications such as walls, barricades, or trenches as a 10-minute activity. These fortifications provide cover or difficult terrain as appropriate. You can have up to three active fortifications at a time. This represents your ability to quickly create defensive positions.  
Master Weapon Smith \[Feat 8\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Weapon Smithing feat  
You can Craft weapons of up to rare rarity without needing a formula. Additionally, weapons you Craft have their critical threat range increased by 2 (for example, a weapon with a ×2 critical multiplier gains an 18-20 critical threat range), and they deal an additional 1 damage on critical hits. This represents your mastery of weapon crafting.  
Trap Specialist \[Feat 10\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Improved Trap Maker feature  
You can create more sophisticated traps with multiple triggers or effects. When you create a trap, you can choose one of the following additional effects:  
The trap restrains creatures that fail their Reflex save  
The trap creates difficult terrain in its area  
The trap has a delayed trigger (activates on your next turn or when a specific condition is met)  
The trap affects a 15-foot burst instead of a 10-foot square  
This represents your advanced knowledge of trap design and construction.  
Levels 11-15:  
Legendary Artisan's Tools \[Feat 12\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Advanced Artisan's Tools feat  
You gain proficiency with a third type of artisan's tools. Additionally, when you use your artisan's tools to Aid yourself or others, the bonus increases to \+4 instead of \+3. This represents your legendary expertise across multiple crafts.  
Master Fortification Builder \[Feat 12\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Fortification Building feat  
You can construct more complex fortifications such as watchtowers, gatehouses, or reinforced walls as a 1-hour activity. These fortifications provide greater protection and can house defenders. You can have up to five active fortifications at a time. This represents your ability to create substantial defensive works.  
Perfect Weapon Smith \[Feat 14\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Master Weapon Smith feat  
You can Craft weapons of up to legendary rarity without needing a formula. Additionally, weapons you Craft have their critical threat range increased by 3 (for example, a weapon with a ×2 critical multiplier gains a 17-20 critical threat range), and they deal an additional 2 damage on critical hits. Finally, weapons you Craft are treated as magical for the purpose of overcoming resistances. This represents your perfect mastery of weapon crafting.  
Trap Master \[Feat 16\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Trap Specialist feat  
You can create extremely sophisticated traps with multiple triggers, effects, and reset mechanisms. When you create a trap, you can choose two of the additional effects from the Trap Specialist feat, and the trap can reset itself after being triggered (allowing it to be triggered multiple times). Additionally, you can have up to seven active traps at a time. This represents your mastery of trap design and construction.  
Levels 16-20:  
Master of All Crafts \[Feat 18\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Legendary Artisan's Tools feat  
You gain proficiency with all types of artisan's tools. Additionally, when you use your artisan's tools to Aid yourself or others, the bonus increases to \+5 instead of \+4. This represents your complete mastery of all crafts.  
Fortress Builder \[Feat 18\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Master Fortification Builder feat  
You can construct entire fortresses or strongholds as a 1-day activity. These fortifications include walls, towers, gates, and internal structures, and they can house large numbers of defenders. This represents your ability to create massive defensive works that can withstand prolonged sieges.  
Legendary Weapon Smith \[Feat 20\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Perfect Weapon Smith feat  
You can Craft weapons that are considered artifacts. These weapons have unique properties and immense power, capable of slaying even the mightiest Nephilim. Once per month, you can spend a week of downtime creating a legendary weapon with properties determined by the GM. This represents your ability to create weapons of mythic power.  
Trap Legend \[Feat 20\]  
Artisan  
Prerequisites Artisan class, Trap Master feat  
You can create traps of legendary complexity and power. When you create a trap, you can choose three of the additional effects from the Trap Specialist feat, and the trap can reset itself instantly after being triggered. Additionally, you can have up to ten active traps at a time, and the damage from your traps increases to 5d6. This represents your legendary mastery of trap design and construction.  
Example Build  
Tubal, Cainite Master Smith — A Cainite Artisan who has dedicated his life to mastering the metallurgical arts passed down from his ancestor Tubal-Cain. With high Intelligence and Strength, he crafts weapons and armor of exceptional quality, creating bronze greatswords that can pierce Nephilim hide and shields that can turn aside titanic blows. His Trap Maker ability allows him to create mechanical traps that can capture or kill giant raiders, while his Structural Weakness feature helps him identify weak points in enemy fortifications. Tubal begins with 1 CP from his Cainite heritage but uses his skills to protect his community from giant oppression. His goal is to create a arsenal of weapons and defenses that can help humanity survive in a world dominated by titans.  
Scribe  
The Scribe stands as the keeper of antediluvian knowledge, a scholar and tactician who wields lore and analysis as weapons in the struggle against corruption and giant oppression. Unlike the martial prowess of the Warrior, the divine power of the Priest, or the practical skills of the Artisan, the Scribe's strength lies in their intellect, their memory, and their ability to understand and manipulate the world through knowledge. They are the record-keepers who preserve the wisdom of past generations, the strategists who plan campaigns against Nephilim warlords, and the scholars who decipher forbidden texts and ancient prophecies.  
Scribes trace their lineage back to Penemue, the Watcher who taught humanity writing and ink, though many come from Sethite or Cainite backgrounds where literacy and record-keeping are valued. They are the inheritors of the first written traditions, preserving knowledge on clay tablets, parchment scrolls, and other media that can survive the ravages of time. Some Scribes serve in temple libraries, maintaining sacred texts and genealogies. Others work as court scholars for tribal leaders or city governors, providing counsel and recording important events. A few are wandering scholars who travel between settlements, collecting knowledge and sharing it with those who need it.  
In society, Scribes occupy a position of intellectual authority and cultural importance. Their ability to read and write sets them apart from the majority of the population, giving them access to knowledge that others cannot obtain. They are often called upon to mediate disputes, interpret ancient laws, and provide historical context for current events. Their records become the foundation of tribal memory and cultural identity, preserving the stories, laws, and wisdom of past generations for future use.  
The Scribe's approach to knowledge reflects the bronze-age setting and the critical importance of information in a world without mass communication or permanent records. They do not study abstract academic subjects but practical knowledge that can help their communities survive and thrive—genealogies that establish legitimate leadership, astronomical observations that predict seasons and celestial events, medical texts that describe healing techniques, and strategic analyses that can defeat numerically superior foes. Their work requires patience, attention to detail, and a deep understanding of the patterns and connections that underlie seemingly unrelated events.  
The central feature of the Scribe class is their ability to use knowledge as a weapon. Unlike spellcasters who rely on supernatural power or warriors who depend on physical strength, Scribes use their understanding of the world to gain advantages in combat and social situations. They can analyze an opponent's fighting style and identify weaknesses, decipher ancient texts to uncover forgotten secrets, or use their knowledge of history and politics to manipulate social situations to their advantage. This intellectual approach makes them invaluable members of any adventuring party, providing insights and strategies that can overcome challenges that would defeat purely physical or magical approaches.  
Class Statistics  
Key Ability: Intelligence  
Hit Points: 6 plus your Constitution modifier  
Initial Proficiencies:  
Perception: Trained  
Saving Throws: Fortitude Trained, Reflex Trained, Will Expert  
Skills: Trained in Society and four other skills of your choice  
Attacks: Trained in simple weapons and unarmed attacks  
Defenses: Trained in unarmored defense  
Class DC: Trained in Will DC  
Class Features  
Levels 1-5: At 1st level, Scribes gain Lorekeeper, granting them a \+1 circumstance bonus to all checks to Recall Knowledge. They also gain the Society skill as trained if they don't already have it. At 2nd level, Ancient Languages allows them to read and understand any written language, including ancient scripts, celestial writing, and forbidden texts. They can also decipher codes and ciphers with a successful check. At 3rd level, Tactical Analysis allows them to study an opponent and identify weaknesses. As a single action, they can make a check to Recall Knowledge about a creature they can see. On a success, they learn one of the creature's weaknesses, resistances, or vulnerabilities. On a critical success, they learn two such traits. At 4th level, Prophetic Warning grants them limited precognition. Once per day, they can attempt a DC 15 flat check. On a success, they receive a vague but helpful warning about an upcoming danger or opportunity. At 5th level, Extra Attack enables two attacks when using the Attack action (though Scribes rarely engage in direct combat).  
Levels 6-10: At 6th level, Scribes gain Master Lorekeeper, increasing their circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge to \+2. At 7th level, Enhanced Ancient Languages allows them to not only read any written language but also speak and write it fluently. They can also communicate telepathically with creatures that share a language with them. At 8th level, Improved Tactical Analysis allows them to use their Tactical Analysis ability twice per day instead of once per day. Additionally, on a critical success, they learn three traits about the creature instead of two. At 9th level, Combat Mastery allows them to make an additional attack when using the Attack action, though this third attack takes a \-5 penalty. At 10th level, Strategic Insight allows them to grant tactical advice to allies. Once per day, as a single action, they can grant an ally within 30 feet a \+2 status bonus to their next attack roll or skill check.  
Levels 11-15: At 11th level, Scribes gain Legendary Lorekeeper, increasing their circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge to \+3. At 12th level, Perfect Ancient Languages allows them to understand the intent and meaning behind any written communication, even if it is deliberately misleading or coded. They can also detect lies in written form with a successful check. At 13th level, Supreme Tactical Analysis allows them to use their Tactical Analysis ability three times per day instead of once per day. Additionally, on a critical success, they learn four traits about the creature instead of three. At 14th level, Master Strategist allows them to grant tactical advice to multiple allies. Once per day, as a single action, they can grant all allies within 30 feet a \+2 status bonus to their next attack roll or skill check. At 15th level, Prophetic Vision enhances their precognitive abilities. They can use their Prophetic Warning ability twice per day instead of once per day, and the warnings are more specific and detailed.  
Levels 16-20: At 16th level, Scribes gain Perfect Lorekeeper, increasing their circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge to \+4. At 17th level, Telepathic Communication allows them to communicate telepathically with any creature within 120 feet, regardless of language barriers. This communication is two-way and can convey complex ideas and emotions. At 18th level, Perfect Tactical Analysis allows them to use their Tactical Analysis ability at will. Additionally, on a critical success, they learn all of the creature's weaknesses, resistances, and vulnerabilities. At 19th level, Martial Mastery allows them to make four attacks when using the Attack action, with the fourth attack taking a \-10 penalty. At 20th level, Oracle of Knowledge grants them near-omniscient understanding. They can automatically succeed at any check to Recall Knowledge, and they can use their Prophetic Warning ability at will. Additionally, they can communicate telepathically with any creature on the same plane of existence.

Class Feats  
Levels 1-5:  
Decipher Script \[Feat 1\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class  
You can decipher magical writing and ancient scripts that would be incomprehensible to others. You can use your Ancient Languages ability to identify the properties and effects of magical scrolls, tomes, and inscriptions. This represents your deep understanding of written communication in all its forms.  
Knowledge is Power \[Feat 1\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Intelligence 14+  
Once per day, you can spend 10 minutes studying a topic to gain expertise in it. For the next hour, you are considered trained in any skill related to that topic, even if you don't normally have that skill. This represents your ability to quickly absorb and apply knowledge.  
Multilingual \[Feat 1\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class  
You learn two additional languages of your choice. These can be any languages, including regional languages, secret languages, or even celestial and abyssal tongues. This represents your broad linguistic knowledge and ability to communicate with diverse groups.  
Researcher \[Feat 4\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Lorekeeper feature  
You can research topics quickly and efficiently. When you spend downtime researching a topic, you gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks related to that research, and you can complete the research in half the normal time. This represents your efficient research methods and access to information sources.  
Levels 6-10:  
Ancient Lore \[Feat 6\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Ancient Languages feature  
You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about ancient history, lost civilizations, and forgotten knowledge. Additionally, you can identify artifacts and relics from ancient times with a successful check. This represents your specialized knowledge of the distant past.  
Battlefield Analyst \[Feat 6\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Tactical Analysis feature  
You can analyze battlefield conditions and terrain to identify tactical advantages. As a single action, you can make a check to Recall Knowledge about the terrain you are in. On a success, you and all allies within 30 feet gain a \+1 status bonus to attack rolls and AC for 1 minute. On a critical success, this bonus increases to \+2. This represents your ability to use terrain to your advantage.  
Linguistic Mastery \[Feat 8\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Multilingual feat  
You learn four additional languages of your choice. Additionally, you can understand the nuances and cultural context of any language you know, allowing you to communicate more effectively and avoid misunderstandings. This represents your deep understanding of language and culture.  
Tactical Genius \[Feat 10\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Strategic Insight feature  
You can develop complex tactical plans that coordinate multiple allies. Once per day, as a 10-minute activity, you can create a tactical plan that grants all allies who follow it a \+2 status bonus to attack rolls and skill checks for 1 hour. This represents your ability to coordinate complex operations.  
Levels 11-15:  
Forbidden Knowledge \[Feat 12\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Ancient Lore feat  
You can safely study and understand forbidden texts and corrupted knowledge without suffering the normal spiritual consequences. You gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about forbidden arts, Watcher teachings, and corrupted practices. This represents your ability to study dangerous knowledge without being corrupted by it.  
Master Battlefield Analyst \[Feat 12\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Battlefield Analyst feat  
Your battlefield analysis abilities improve. The status bonus from your Battlefield Analyst ability increases to \+2 on a success and \+3 on a critical success. Additionally, the duration increases to 10 minutes. This represents your enhanced ability to analyze and exploit battlefield conditions.  
Perfect Linguist \[Feat 14\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Linguistic Mastery feat  
You learn all languages spoken in your region of the world. Additionally, you can understand and communicate with creatures that don't use language through gestures, expressions, and other non-verbal means. This represents your perfect understanding of communication in all its forms.  
Strategic Mastermind \[Feat 16\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Tactical Genius feat  
You can develop strategic plans that can turn the tide of entire campaigns. Once per week, as a 1-hour activity, you can create a strategic plan that grants all allies who follow it a \+3 status bonus to attack rolls and skill checks for 8 hours. This represents your ability to coordinate large-scale operations.  
Levels 16-20:  
Absolute Knowledge \[Feat 18\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Perfect Lorekeeper feature  
You can automatically succeed at any check to Recall Knowledge about topics you have studied or encountered before. Additionally, you can attempt checks to Recall Knowledge about topics you have never encountered, with a \-5 penalty. This represents your near-perfect memory and encyclopedic knowledge.  
Master of Forbidden Lore \[Feat 18\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Forbidden Knowledge feat  
You gain a \+4 circumstance bonus to checks to Recall Knowledge about forbidden arts, Watcher teachings, and corrupted practices. Additionally, you can identify the specific Watcher who taught a particular forbidden art or practice with a successful check. This represents your deep understanding of corrupted knowledge.  
Perfect Strategist \[Feat 20\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Strategic Mastermind feat  
You can develop perfect strategic plans that account for every variable and contingency. Once per week, as a 1-hour activity, you can create a strategic plan that grants all allies who follow it a \+4 status bonus to attack rolls and skill checks for 24 hours. Additionally, enemies who oppose the plan take a \-2 penalty to attack rolls and skill checks. This represents your ability to create flawless strategic plans.  
Telepathic Network \[Feat 20\]  
Scribe  
Prerequisites Scribe class, Telepathic Communication feature  
You can create a telepathic network linking multiple creatures. Once per day, as a 10-minute activity, you can link up to ten willing creatures into a telepathic network. All creatures in the network can communicate telepathically with each other, share sensory information, and coordinate their actions perfectly. The network lasts for 1 hour. This represents your ability to create perfect communication and coordination between allies.  
Example Build  
Enoch, Keeper of Scrolls — A Sethite Scribe who has dedicated his life to preserving ancient knowledge and deciphering prophecies about the coming judgment. With high Intelligence and Wisdom, he uses his Lorekeeper ability to recall crucial information about ancient texts and forbidden practices, while his Tactical Analysis feature helps him identify weaknesses in Nephilim warlords and corrupted beings. His Prophetic Warning ability provides crucial insights that help his companions avoid danger and seize opportunities. Enoch begins with 2 RP from his Sethite heritage, which he uses to fuel his righteous pursuits and resist the temptation of forbidden knowledge. His goal is to compile a complete record of antediluvian history and prophecy before the Flood destroys all knowledge, ensuring that future generations will understand what happened and why.

Chapter 5: The Soul Economy  
Introduction  
In the antediluvian world, every choice carries eternal weight. Unlike standard fantasy settings where morality is abstract or inconsequential, Nephilim Wars introduces the Soul Economy—a unique resource system that tracks your character's spiritual state through Righteousness Points (RP) and Corruption Points (CP). This is not merely a roleplaying suggestion or narrative flavor; it is a core mechanical subsystem that directly impacts your capabilities, social interactions, and ultimate fate in a world where divine judgment looms over all.  
The Soul Economy reflects the fundamental tension of the antediluvian age: the struggle between righteousness and corruption, between the divine wisdom preserved by the righteous line and the forbidden knowledge taught by the fallen Watchers. Your Net Righteousness Score (RP minus CP) determines whether you stand favored by the Most High, marked for judgment, or precariously balanced on the edge between salvation and damnation.  
This system creates meaningful gameplay opportunities that go beyond simple combat and skill challenges. Do you use forbidden Watcher magic to defeat a Nephilim warlord, knowing each spell risks corrupting your soul? Do you show mercy to a defeated enemy when your allies demand execution? Do you keep your sacred oaths even when breaking them would save lives? These are not abstract moral dilemmas—they have immediate mechanical consequences that shape your character's journey and the campaign's narrative.  
The Soul Economy also serves as a campaign pacing mechanism. As characters accumulate Corruption Points, they face increasing mechanical penalties and narrative consequences, creating natural tension as the Flood approaches. Conversely, characters who maintain high Righteousness Scores gain advantages that help them survive the increasingly dangerous world, but they must constantly resist the temptations of forbidden power that surround them.  
This chapter provides complete rules for the Soul Economy, including how to gain and spend RP and CP, the mechanical effects of your Net Righteousness Score, character retirement conditions, and examples of the system in play. Whether you are a player seeking to understand how your choices affect your character, or a Game Master looking to integrate this system into your campaign, this chapter provides everything you need to make the Soul Economy a central and compelling feature of your Nephilim Wars experience.  
Righteousness Points (RP)  
What Are Righteousness Points?  
Righteousness Points represent your character's spiritual connection to the Most High, their moral integrity, and their alignment with divine will. RP are not merely "good points" in a simplistic morality system—they reflect genuine acts of righteousness, self-sacrifice, faithfulness to covenants, and resistance to corruption in a world that constantly tempts characters toward darkness.  
Characters begin with a baseline number of RP based on their ancestry. Sethites start with 2 RP, reflecting their blessed heritage as descendants of the righteous line. Other ancestries begin with 0 RP, though certain backgrounds and class features may grant additional starting RP. Throughout play, characters can gain RP through righteous actions and spend RP to fuel divine abilities, reroll failures, and resist corruption.  
Gaining Righteousness Points  
Righteousness Points are earned through meaningful actions that demonstrate your character's commitment to righteousness. These are not minor gestures or routine behaviors—they are significant choices that require genuine sacrifice, courage, or faithfulness. The Game Master awards RP at their discretion, typically at the end of a session or after a major story event. The following categories provide guidelines for when RP should be awarded:  
Acts of Genuine Righteousness  
These are actions that demonstrate your character's commitment to moral principles, even when it is difficult or dangerous to do so.  
Protecting the Innocent: When you protect vulnerable individuals from harm, especially when doing so puts you at personal risk. Examples include:  
Shielding villagers from Nephilim raiders  
Rescuing prisoners from sacrifice or slavery  
Defending children, elderly, or disabled individuals from violence  
Standing up for the oppressed against powerful oppressors  
Showing Mercy: When you show mercy to defeated enemies or those who have wronged you, especially when vengeance would be easier or more satisfying. Examples include:  
Sparing a surrendered Nephilim warrior who promises to leave the area  
Forgiving a betrayer who genuinely repents  
Refusing to execute prisoners when your allies demand it  
Offering healing to wounded enemies after a battle  
Keeping Sacred Covenants: When you honor your word and keep sacred promises, even when breaking them would be advantageous. Examples include:  
Fulfilling a vow to protect someone, even at great personal cost  
Honoring agreements with allies, even when they become inconvenient  
Maintaining oaths of loyalty to righteous leaders  
Keeping promises made to the Most High or in sacred ceremonies  
Resisting Corruption: When you actively resist temptations to use forbidden knowledge or engage in corrupt practices. Examples include:  
Refusing to use Watcher-taught magic when it would be convenient  
Rejecting offers of power from corrupted sources  
Avoiding participation in dark rituals or sacrifices  
Resisting the urge to use violence or cruelty when other options exist  
Self-Sacrifice for Others  
These are actions where you willingly suffer harm, loss, or disadvantage to benefit others or advance a righteous cause.  
Physical Sacrifice: When you endure physical harm or risk your life to help others. Examples include:  
Taking a blow meant for an ally  
Volunteering for dangerous missions to protect others  
Enduring torture or imprisonment rather than betraying righteous allies  
Using your own vitality to heal others through divine power  
Material Sacrifice: When you give up valuable resources, wealth, or possessions for righteous purposes. Examples include:  
Donating treasure to help impoverished communities  
Sharing scarce resources with those in greater need  
Giving up powerful weapons or armor to those who need them more  
Refusing payment for righteous deeds  
Social Sacrifice: When you endure social consequences, rejection, or isolation for standing by your principles. Examples include:  
Defending unpopular righteous causes despite social pressure  
Refusing to participate in corrupt practices even when expected by your community  
Standing with outcasts or marginalized groups  
Speaking truth to power despite the risks  
Faithfulness in Adversity  
These are actions that demonstrate unwavering commitment to righteousness even in the face of overwhelming opposition or temptation.  
Maintaining Faith: When you continue to trust in the Most High and follow righteous paths despite apparent abandonment or hardship. Examples include:  
Continuing to pray and seek divine guidance even when prayers seem unanswered  
Maintaining righteous practices in corrupt environments  
Encouraging others to remain faithful during times of trial  
Refusing to curse or blame the Most High for suffering  
Persevering in Righteousness: When you continue to do what is right even when it seems futile or when corruption appears to be winning. Examples include:  
Continuing to fight against Nephilim oppression despite repeated defeats  
Maintaining moral standards even when everyone around you has abandoned them  
Refusing to compromise your principles even when doing so would ensure survival  
Working to preserve knowledge and righteousness even as the world descends into chaos  
Spending Righteousness Points  
Righteousness Points can be spent to fuel divine abilities, overcome challenges, and resist corruption. The following are the primary ways characters can spend RP:  
Rerolling Failed Rolls  
You can spend 1 RP to reroll any failed attack roll, skill check, or saving throw. You must use the result of the second roll, even if it is worse. This represents divine assistance or the strength that comes from righteous living.  
Example: Kedar the Warrior fails a saving throw against a Nephilim's fear aura. He spends 1 RP to reroll the save, trusting in his righteous heritage to overcome the supernatural terror.  
Healing Allies  
You can spend RP to heal yourself or allies through divine power. The amount of healing depends on your character's level and the amount of RP spent:  
Spend 1 RP: Restore 1d8 \+ your level Hit Points to one willing creature  
Spend 2 RP: Restore 2d8 \+ your level Hit Points to one willing creature, or 1d8 \+ your level Hit Points to up to three willing creatures  
Spend 3 RP: Restore 3d8 \+ your level Hit Points to one willing creature, or 2d8 \+ your level Hit Points to up to three willing creatures, or 1d8 \+ your level Hit Points to up to six willing creatures  
This healing can be performed as a single action and does not require any specific class features, though characters with divine classes may have additional healing abilities that synergize with RP spending.  
Example: Tamar the Hunter is gravely wounded after an ambush by Rephaim scouts. Enoch the Priest spends 2 RP to restore 2d8 \+ 7 Hit Points to her, calling upon divine power to mend her wounds.  
Resisting Corruption Effects  
You can spend RP to resist or mitigate the effects of corruption, possession, or other spiritually harmful influences.  
Spend 1 RP: Gain a \+2 status bonus to a saving throw against a corruption effect, possession attempt, or fear effect  
Spend 2 RP: Automatically succeed at a saving throw against a corruption effect or possession attempt of 5th level or lower  
Spend 3 RP: Remove one corruption effect or condition from yourself or an ally (such as being frightened, sickened by corruption, or partially possessed)  
Example: Rahab the Gibbor is targeted by a corrupted sorcerer's possession spell. She spends 2 RP to automatically succeed at the saving throw, her righteous bloodline rejecting the dark magic.  
Powering Divine Miracles  
Characters with divine classes (such as Priests) can spend RP to fuel their divine miracles. The specific costs and effects depend on the miracle being used, but generally:  
Minor Miracles (healing, protection, guidance): 0-1 RP  
Major Miracles (turning undead, divine judgment, sanctuary): 1-2 RP  
Legendary Miracles (resurrection, divine intervention, avatar form): 3+ RP  
These costs are in addition to any spell slots or focus points required by the class's normal mechanics.  
Example: Enoch the Priest uses his Divine Judgment miracle to smite a corrupted Nephilim champion. The miracle normally costs 1 Focus Point, but he spends an additional 2 RP to increase the damage and ensure the corruption is purged from the area.  
Special Actions  
Certain class features, ancestry feats, and background abilities allow characters to spend RP for unique effects. These are detailed in their respective chapters, but common examples include:  
Warrior's Righteous Strike: Spend 1 RP to deal an additional 2d6 radiant damage on your next attack  
Hunter's Divine Guidance: Spend 1 RP to gain a \+2 status bonus to your next Survival or Perception check  
Scribe's Prophetic Insight: Spend 2 RP to receive a vision of an upcoming danger or opportunity  
RP Caps and Recovery  
There is no strict maximum limit on how many RP a character can accumulate, though practical limits exist based on opportunities to earn them. Characters cannot have negative RP—when your RP would be reduced below 0, it remains at 0 instead.  
RP are not automatically recovered between sessions. They represent spiritual capital earned through righteous living, and they must be continually replenished through ongoing righteous actions. However, characters can recover RP through specific means:  
Downtime Devotion: During downtime, a character can spend a week in prayer, meditation, and righteous service to recover 1 RP  
Divine Intervention: In rare cases, a character may receive divine intervention that restores RP (typically 1-2 RP)  
Righteous Community: Characters who are part of a righteous community (such as a Sethite enclave) may recover 1 RP per month simply by maintaining their membership and participating in community worship  
Corruption Points (CP)  
What Are Corruption Points?  
Corruption Points represent your character's spiritual degradation, moral compromise, and alignment with forbidden powers. CP are not merely "evil points" in a simplistic morality system—they reflect genuine corruption of the soul through the use of forbidden knowledge, acts of cruelty, breaking sacred oaths, and consorting with fallen beings.  
Characters begin with a baseline number of CP based on their ancestry. Nephilim start with 3 CP, reflecting their tainted bloodline as offspring of fallen angels. Other giant lineages start with 1-2 CP, while human lineages typically begin with 0-1 CP. Certain backgrounds and class features may grant additional starting CP. Throughout play, characters can gain CP through corrupt actions and may (with difficulty) remove CP through repentance and righteous living.  
Unlike RP, which are earned through positive actions, CP are often gained involuntarily through the use of certain abilities or exposure to corrupting influences. This reflects the insidious nature of corruption in the antediluvian world—sometimes, simply surviving requires compromises that damage the soul.  
Gaining Corruption Points  
Corruption Points are gained through actions that align with forbidden knowledge, cruelty, betrayal, or consorting with corrupted beings. The Game Master tracks CP and informs players when their characters gain them. The following categories provide guidelines for when CP should be gained:  
Using Forbidden Watcher Magic  
The most common source of CP is the use of forbidden magic taught by the fallen Watchers. Each time a character casts a spell from the forbidden traditions (Azazel's warfare magic, Semyaza's enchantments, Baraqel's divinations, etc.), they risk gaining CP. The exact risk depends on the spell's level and nature:  
Spell Level CP Risk:  
Cantrips and 1st-level spells: 10% chance to gain 1 CP (DC 11 flat check)  
2nd-3rd level spells: 20% chance to gain 1 CP (DC 13 flat check)  
4th-5th level spells: 30% chance to gain 1 CP (DC 15 flat check)  
6th-7th level spells: 40% chance to gain 1 CP (DC 17 flat check)  
8th-10th level spells: 50% chance to gain 1 CP (DC 19 flat check)  
Nature of Spell CP Modifiers:  
Harmful spells (damage, curses, control): \+10% to CP risk  
Healing/beneficial spells from forbidden traditions: No modifier  
Divination spells: \-10% to CP risk (knowledge itself is not inherently corrupting)  
Necromancy spells: \+20% to CP risk (directly opposed to divine life)  
Characters with the Sorcerer Clan ancestry or Magi class may have abilities that reduce this CP risk, but they can never eliminate it entirely. The use of forbidden knowledge always carries spiritual consequences.  
Example: Keturah the Magi casts Azazel's Flame (a 3rd-level fire spell) to destroy a Nephilim warband. The spell has a 20% CP risk, modified by \+10% because it's a harmful spell, for a total 30% risk (DC 15 flat check). She fails the check and gains 1 CP, feeling a cold darkness settle in her soul as the flames consume her enemies.  
Acts of Violence and Cruelty  
These are actions that demonstrate a willingness to inflict harm, suffering, or death beyond what is necessary or justified.  
Unnecessary Violence: When you use violence when non-violent alternatives exist, or when you use excessive force beyond what is needed to achieve your goals. Examples include:  
Killing surrendered enemies who pose no threat  
Torturing prisoners for information or pleasure  
Destroying property or resources unnecessarily  
Using intimidation or threats when persuasion would suffice  
Cruelty and Sadism: When you take pleasure in the suffering of others or deliberately cause pain beyond what is necessary. Examples include:  
Mutilating corpses or trophies  
Taunting or humiliating defeated enemies  
Taking joy in the pain of others  
Using psychological torture or manipulation  
Betrayal and Treachery: When you betray trust, break alliances, or act treacherously toward those who have shown you loyalty. Examples include:  
Betraying allies for personal gain  
Breaking sacred oaths or covenants  
Lying to or deceiving those who trust you  
Abandoning allies in danger  
Breaking Sacred Oaths  
These are violations of solemn promises, covenants, or vows that have spiritual significance.  
Breaking Vows: When you deliberately violate promises made in sacred contexts or to the Most High. Examples include:  
Breaking marriage vows or family commitments  
Violating oaths of loyalty to righteous leaders  
Breaking promises made during religious ceremonies  
Failing to fulfill vows made in prayer or sacrifice  
Desecration: When you show disrespect or contempt for sacred places, objects, or practices. Examples include:  
Defiling temples or places of worship  
Destroying sacred texts or relics  
Mocking religious practices or beliefs  
Using sacred objects for corrupt purposes  
Consorting with Fallen Beings  
These are actions that involve cooperation, alliance, or fellowship with corrupted entities.  
Alliance with Corrupted Beings: When you form partnerships, alliances, or friendships with beings that are known to be corrupted. Examples include:  
Working with Nephilim or other giant lineages against human interests  
Forming pacts with demons or disembodied spirits (Refa'im)  
Cooperating with Watcher cults or corrupted sorcerers  
Accepting aid or gifts from known corrupted sources  
Participating in Corrupt Practices: When you engage in rituals, ceremonies, or practices that are known to be corrupt or forbidden. Examples include:  
Participating in blood sacrifices or dark rituals  
Using forbidden knowledge for personal gain  
Engaging in practices taught by the Watchers without righteous purpose  
Seeking power from corrupted sources  
Mechanical Effects of Corruption Points  
Corruption Points have immediate and cumulative mechanical effects that represent the spiritual degradation of your character. These effects are always active and cannot be ignored or suppressed—they are visible manifestations of your soul's condition.  
Initiative Penalty  
Your Corruption Points reduce your Initiative modifier, representing a sluggish spirit weighed down by sin and corruption.  
Initiative Penalty: Subtract your total CP from your Initiative modifier.  
Example: Rahab the Gibbor has 4 CP. Her normal Initiative modifier is \+5 (from her Dexterity), but with the CP penalty, her effective Initiative modifier is \+1. In combat, she acts much later than she normally would, her corrupted soul slowing her reactions.  
Divine Judgment Risk  
As your Corruption Points accumulate, you become increasingly vulnerable to divine judgment effects. These are supernatural consequences that manifest when you reach certain CP thresholds:  
3+ CP: You are considered "Corrupted" for the purposes of divine abilities. Priests' Turn Unholy ability affects you as if you were an undead creature. Sacred places may cause you discomfort or prevent you from entering.  
5+ CP: You gain the Marked for Judgment condition. Once per day, when you roll a natural 1 on any d20 roll, you must attempt a DC 15 Will save. On a failure, you suffer a minor divine judgment effect (such as being dazzled for 1 round, taking 1d6 mental damage, or being frightened 1).  
7+ CP: The DC of your Marked for Judgment Will saves increases to 18, and the effects become more severe (such as being stunned for 1 round, taking 2d6 mental damage, or being frightened 2).  
10+ CP: You gain the Condemned condition. Divine judgment effects can now trigger on natural rolls of 1-2 (instead of just 1), and the DC increases to 20\. Additionally, righteous NPCs may refuse to interact with you, and some divine abilities may automatically fail when used on you.  
15+ CP: You are considered Beyond Redemption by most righteous beings. Divine judgment effects trigger on natural rolls of 1-3, and you may be subject to automatic divine punishment in sacred places or when encountering powerful righteous entities.  
Vulnerability to Demonic Influence  
Corrupted characters are more susceptible to possession, influence, and manipulation by demonic entities and disembodied spirits (Refa'im).  
Possession Resistance Penalty: Subtract your total CP from saving throws against possession attempts and mental influence effects from corrupted sources.  
Example: Tamar the Hunter has 3 CP when a disembodied Nephilim spirit attempts to possess her. The possession requires a DC 18 Will save, but her CP penalty reduces her effective save bonus by 3, making it much harder to resist.  
Social Consequences  
Your Corruption Points affect how NPCs react to you, particularly those who are righteous or spiritually sensitive.  
Righteous NPC Reactions: When interacting with NPCs who have 3+ RP or are known to be righteous (such as Sethite elders, priests, or prophets), your Corruption Points impose a penalty on your Diplomacy and Deception checks.  
Penalty: Subtract your total CP from Diplomacy and Deception checks when dealing with righteous NPCs.  
Example: Enoch the Priest has 5 RP and is known for his righteousness. When Keturah the Magi (with 4 CP) tries to persuade him to join her in using forbidden magic, her Diplomacy check is penalized by 4, making it nearly impossible to convince him.  
Corrupted NPC Bonuses: Conversely, when dealing with corrupted NPCs (those with 3+ CP or known to be corrupted), your Corruption Points may grant bonuses to Intimidation and Deception checks.  
Bonus: Add half your total CP (rounded down) to Intimidation and Deception checks when dealing with corrupted NPCs.  
Example: Keturah (4 CP) attempts to intimidate a Nephilim warlord (who has 6 CP). She adds \+2 to her Intimidation check, her corruption resonating with his own dark nature.  
Removing Corruption Points  
Unlike RP, which are earned through positive actions, CP are difficult to remove. Corruption represents spiritual damage that cannot be easily healed. However, there are several ways characters can reduce their CP:  
Repentance and Atonement  
The most common way to remove CP is through genuine repentance and atonement. This requires:  
Acknowledgment: The character must genuinely acknowledge their corruption and express remorse for their actions  
Restitution: The character must make amends for their corrupt actions to the best of their ability  
Change: The character must demonstrate a genuine change in behavior, avoiding further corrupt actions  
When a character completes this process, they can attempt a DC 15 Will save at the end of a session. On a success, they remove 1 CP. On a critical success, they remove 2 CP. This can only be attempted once per week.  
Example: After using forbidden magic to defeat a Nephilim warlord, Keturah realizes the spiritual cost of her actions. She confesses her corruption to Enoch the Priest, seeks forgiveness, and vows to rely on righteous power in the future. At the end of the session, she succeeds at her Will save and removes 1 CP.  
Divine Intervention  
In rare cases, a character may receive divine intervention that removes CP. This typically requires:  
A significant act of righteousness or self-sacrifice  
Intercession by a powerful righteous NPC (such as a prophet or archangel)  
A divine miracle or blessing  
The amount of CP removed depends on the significance of the intervention, typically 1-3 CP.  
Example: Kedar the Warrior sacrifices himself to save a village from a Nephilim raid. As he lies dying, a divine light envelops him, healing his wounds and purging 2 CP from his soul as reward for his selfless act.  
Righteous Community  
Characters who are part of a righteous community (such as a Sethite enclave) may slowly remove CP through participation in community worship and righteous living.  
Weekly Worship: By participating in weekly worship and righteous activities, a character can attempt a DC 12 Will save at the end of each week. On a success, they remove 1 CP.  
Monthly Retreat: By spending a month in a righteous community focused on prayer and repentance, a character can remove 1 CP automatically, or 2 CP on a successful DC 15 Will save.  
These methods are slow but reliable, representing the gradual healing that comes from immersion in righteous community.  
Extreme Measures  
In desperate cases, characters may resort to extreme measures to remove CP:  
Trial by Ordeal: Voluntarily subjecting oneself to a dangerous trial or ordeal as penance. On success, remove 1-2 CP. On failure, gain 1 CP.  
Sacrificial Magic: Using powerful divine magic to forcibly remove CP, at great personal cost (such as permanent ability score reduction or loss of class features).  
Divine Bargain: Making a pact with a divine entity to remove CP in exchange for future service or sacrifice.  
These methods are risky and should be used sparingly, representing the desperate measures that corrupted souls may take to find redemption.  
Net Righteousness Score  
Calculating Your Net Righteousness  
Your Net Righteousness Score is the difference between your Righteousness Points and your Corruption Points:  
Net Righteousness \= RP \- CP  
This score determines your overall spiritual state and has significant mechanical and narrative consequences. Unlike RP and CP individually, which have specific uses and effects, your Net Righteousness Score represents your soul's overall condition in relation to divine judgment.  
Net Righteousness Tiers  
Your Net Righteousness Score falls into one of five tiers, each with distinct mechanical benefits and narrative implications:  
Tier 1: Blessed (Net \+5 or higher)  
Characters in this tier are favored by the Most High, their righteousness shining like a beacon in the darkness of the antediluvian world.  
Mechanical Benefits:  
Divine Favor: Add \+1 status bonus to all attack rolls, skill checks, and saving throws  
Resistance to Corruption: Add \+2 status bonus to saving throws against corruption effects and possession attempts  
Healing Bonus: Healing received from divine sources is increased by 50%  
Divine Protection: Once per day, when you would take damage from a corrupted source, you can reduce that damage by half  
Narrative Implications:  
Righteous NPCs treat you with reverence and respect  
You may receive divine visions, prophecies, or guidance  
Corrupted beings may fear or avoid you  
You are considered a candidate for divine missions or special purposes  
Example: Enoch the Priest maintains a Net Righteousness of \+7 through constant prayer, righteous living, and divine service. His attacks are blessed with divine accuracy, and corrupted beings shrink from his presence.  
Tier 2: Righteous (Net \+1 to \+4)  
Characters in this tier are aligned with divine will and maintain a strong connection to righteousness, though they are not yet at the peak of spiritual purity.  
Mechanical Benefits:  
Righteous Bonus: Add \+1 status bonus to saving throws against fear effects and corruption  
Healing Bonus: Healing received from divine sources is increased by 25%  
Divine Sensitivity: You can sense the presence of corrupted beings within 30 feet (GM informs you when corrupted creatures are nearby)  
Narrative Implications:  
Righteous NPCs trust and respect you  
You are welcomed in righteous communities and sacred places  
You may be asked to take on leadership roles in righteous causes  
Corrupted beings recognize you as an opponent but do not necessarily fear you  
Example: Kedar the Warrior has a Net Righteousness of \+3, earned through protecting his tribe and resisting the temptation of forbidden weapons. He is respected by his people and trusted to lead righteous missions.  
Tier 3: Balanced (Net 0\)  
Characters in this tier are spiritually neutral, neither particularly righteous nor corrupted. They stand on the edge, vulnerable to influences from both sides.  
Mechanical Benefits:  
None — Characters at Net 0 receive no special benefits or penalties from their Net Righteousness  
Narrative Implications:  
Righteous NPCs are cautious but not hostile  
Corrupted NPCs see you as potential recruits or allies  
You have freedom to choose your path without strong divine or corrupt influence  
You may be torn between righteous and corrupt opportunities  
Example: Tamar the Hunter maintains a Net Righteousness of 0, having earned RP through protecting her companions but gained CP through using forbidden tracking magic. She is respected by both righteous and corrupt factions, but fully trusted by neither.  
Tier 4: Corrupted (Net \-1 to \-4)  
Characters in this tier have succumbed to corruption and are aligned with forbidden powers, though they have not yet reached the depths of damnation.  
Mechanical Penalties:  
Corruption Penalty: Subtract 1 from all saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities  
Vulnerability: You take 25% more damage from radiant and divine sources  
Divine Disfavor: Righteous NPCs are suspicious and may refuse to help you  
Narrative Implications:  
Righteous NPCs avoid or oppose you  
Corrupted NPCs welcome and trust you  
You may be offered power and knowledge from corrupted sources  
Sacred places may cause you discomfort or prevent entry  
Example: Keturah the Magi has a Net Righteousness of \-2, having gained CP through frequent use of forbidden magic. Righteous characters are wary of her, but corrupted sorcerers seek her alliance.  
Tier 5: Condemned (Net \-5 or lower)  
Characters in this tier are beyond redemption in the eyes of most righteous beings, their souls so corrupted that they are marked for divine judgment.  
Mechanical Penalties:  
Condemnation Penalty: Subtract 2 from all saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities  
Vulnerability: You take 50% more damage from radiant and divine sources  
Divine Hostility: Righteous NPCs may attack you on sight or refuse all interaction  
Judgment Vulnerability: Divine judgment effects trigger more frequently and are more severe  
Narrative Implications:  
Righteous NPCs consider you an enemy to be destroyed  
You are barred from sacred places and righteous communities  
Corrupted NPCs see you as a powerful ally or leader  
You may be targeted by divine agents or righteous champions  
The Flood may come for you specifically  
Example: A Nephilim warlord has a Net Righteousness of \-8, his soul corrupted by centuries of tyranny and forbidden power. Righteous characters see him as a monster to be slain, while his corrupted followers worship him as a god.  
Shifting Tiers  
Your Net Righteousness Tier can change as your RP and CP fluctuate. When your Net Righteousness crosses a tier boundary, the mechanical benefits and penalties change immediately. This can create dramatic shifts in your character's capabilities and social standing.  
Example: Keturah the Magi has a Net Righteousness of \+1 (Righteous tier) after a session of righteous deeds. During the next session, she uses forbidden magic three times, gaining 3 CP and dropping her Net Righteousness to \-2 (Corrupted tier). Immediately, she loses her Righteous Bonus and gains the Corruption Penalty, and righteous NPCs who were friendly become suspicious.  
Tier Transitions and Character Development  
Significant shifts in Net Righteousness Tier should be accompanied by character development and narrative consequences. Moving from Corrupted to Righteous (or vice versa) represents a fundamental change in your character's spiritual state and should be reflected in their behavior, relationships, and story arc.  
Redemption Arc: A character moving from Corrupted to Righteous should demonstrate genuine change, repentance, and growth. This may involve:  
Acknowledging past wrongs and making amends  
Rejecting corrupt allies and practices  
Embracing righteous causes and communities  
Struggling with lingering corruption and temptation  
Fall from Grace: A character moving from Righteous to Corrupted should demonstrate moral decline, compromise, and corruption. This may involve:  
Justifying increasingly corrupt actions  
Abandoning righteous principles for power or convenience  
Forming alliances with corrupted beings  
Resisting attempts at redemption or intervention  
These transitions provide rich roleplaying opportunities and can be central to your character's story in the campaign.  
Character Retirement Conditions  
The Coming Judgment  
In the antediluvian world, divine judgment is not an abstract concept—it is an imminent reality. The Flood is coming, and every soul will be judged. This creates natural character retirement conditions based on your Net Righteousness Score and the campaign's progression toward the Flood.  
Righteous Retirement  
Characters who maintain a high Net Righteousness Score (+5 or higher) throughout the campaign may be spared from the Flood through divine intervention. This represents the biblical precedent of Noah and his family being saved from the Deluge.  
Conditions for Righteous Retirement:  
Maintain Net Righteousness of \+5 or higher for at least the final three sessions of the campaign  
Complete at least one major righteous quest or mission (such as protecting Noah's family, preserving sacred knowledge, or defeating a major corrupted threat)  
Demonstrate genuine faith and commitment to righteousness, not just mechanical RP accumulation  
Retirement Benefits:  
Your character survives the Flood and becomes part of the post-diluvian world  
You may create a new character who is a descendant of your retired character  
Your retired character may appear as an NPC or mentor in future campaigns  
You gain a legacy bonus: \+1 to all ability scores for your next character  
Example: Enoch the Priest maintains a Net Righteousness of \+7 throughout the campaign, protecting his community and preserving sacred knowledge. When the Flood comes, he is taken up to heaven like the biblical Enoch, his righteousness sparing him from destruction.  
Corrupted Retirement  
Characters who accumulate high Corruption Points and low Net Righteousness are marked for judgment and will not survive the Flood. This represents the biblical destruction of the corrupt world.  
Conditions for Corrupted Retirement:  
Reach Net Righteousness of \-5 or lower and maintain it for at least two sessions  
Commit at least one major corrupt act (such as betraying righteous allies, serving a Watcher, or leading a corrupt community)  
Reject opportunities for redemption or repentance  
Retirement Consequences:  
Your character perishes in the Flood or is destroyed by divine judgment before the Flood  
You may create a new character who is unrelated to your retired character  
Your retired character may appear as an antagonist or cautionary tale in future campaigns  
You gain a corruption legacy: Your next character begins with 1 bonus CP but also gains access to one corrupted ability or feat  
Example: A Nephilim warlord maintains a Net Righteousness of \-8, ruling through tyranny and forbidden power. When the Flood comes, he is destroyed along with his corrupt kingdom, his soul condemned to wander as a disembodied spirit (Refa'im).  
Balanced Retirement  
Characters who remain spiritually neutral (Net Righteousness between \-4 and \+4) face an uncertain fate. They are not specifically spared like the righteous, nor are they specifically condemned like the corrupted. Their survival depends on circumstances and choices made at the end of the campaign.  
Conditions for Balanced Retirement:  
Maintain Net Righteousness between \-4 and \+4 throughout most of the campaign  
Avoid major righteous or corrupt acts that would shift your tier significantly  
Make a final choice at the campaign's climax that determines your fate  
Retirement Options:  
Choose Righteousness: In the final session, make a significant righteous choice (such as sacrificing yourself to save others, rejecting corruption, or aiding righteous causes). If successful, survive the Flood with a legacy bonus.  
Choose Corruption: In the final session, make a significant corrupt choice (such as betraying allies, embracing forbidden power, or serving corrupted beings). If successful, perish in the Flood but gain a corruption legacy.  
Remain Neutral: Make no significant choice, remaining balanced. Your fate is determined by the GM based on your actions and the campaign's needs. You may survive through luck, be destroyed as collateral damage, or face an ambiguous fate.  
Example: Tamar the Hunter maintains a Net Righteousness of 0 throughout the campaign, helping both righteous and corrupt factions when it serves her goals. In the final session, she must choose whether to help Noah build the Ark (righteous path) or join a Nephilim warlord's last stand (corrupt path). Her choice determines her fate in the Flood.  
Early Retirement  
Characters may retire before the campaign's end for various reasons:  
Voluntary Retirement:  
A character may choose to retire to pursue a different path (such as becoming a hermit, joining a monastery, or starting a family)  
The player creates a new character to continue in the campaign  
The retired character may appear as an NPC in future sessions  
Forced Retirement:  
A character may be forced to retire due to death, incapacitation, or other circumstances  
If the retirement is due to corruption (such as possession or transformation into a monster), the character may become an antagonist  
If the retirement is due to righteousness (such as ascension or divine calling), the character may become a mentor or ally  
Legacy System:  
Retired characters leave a legacy that affects future characters  
Righteous legacies provide bonuses to righteous actions and RP gain  
Corrupted legacies provide bonuses to corrupt actions and access to forbidden abilities  
Balanced legacies provide flexibility but no specific bonuses  
Sample Scenarios  
Scenario 1: The Temptation of Power  
Situation: The party faces a Nephilim warlord who is terrorizing a village. The warlord is too powerful for the party to defeat through conventional means. Keturah the Magi knows a forbidden spell (Azazel's Flame, 4th level) that could destroy the warlord, but it carries a 40% risk of gaining 1 CP.  
Player Choices:  
Use the spell: Keturah casts Azazel's Flame, dealing massive damage to the warlord. She fails the CP risk check and gains 1 CP. The village is saved, but her soul is corrupted.  
Refuse the spell: Keturah refuses to use forbidden magic, suggesting alternative tactics. The party must find another way to defeat the warlord, possibly through stealth, diplomacy, or seeking help from righteous allies.  
Compromise: Keturah uses a weaker, non-corrupting spell to weaken the warlord, then the party attacks conventionally. This takes longer and is riskier, but preserves her righteousness.  
Consequences:  
Using the spell gains immediate victory but long-term corruption  
Refusing the spell maintains righteousness but risks failure  
Compromising balances immediate needs with long-term consequences  
Scenario 2: Mercy vs. Justice  
Situation: The party captures a Nephilim scout who was part of a raid that killed several villagers. The villagers demand execution, but the scout surrenders and promises to leave the area forever.  
Player Choices:  
Execute the scout: The party executes the scout, satisfying the villagers but gaining 1 CP each for unnecessary violence. The villagers are grateful, but righteous NPCs are disturbed by the brutality.  
Spare the scout: The party spares the scout, gaining 1 RP each for showing mercy. The villagers are angry and may refuse future help, but righteous NPCs respect the decision.  
Compromise: The party spares the scout but demands restitution (such as returning stolen goods or providing information). This gains no RP or CP but maintains relationships with both villagers and righteous NPCs.  
Consequences:  
Execution provides immediate satisfaction but spiritual cost  
Mercy provides spiritual benefit but social cost  
Compromise maintains balance but may not fully satisfy either side  
Scenario 3: The Corrupted Ally  
Situation: A party member (Keturah) has accumulated 5 CP and is showing signs of corruption (initiative penalty, vulnerability to possession). The other players must decide how to respond.  
Player Choices:
