Chapter 10: Playing the Game  
Introduction: The Three Modes of Play  
In Nephilim Wars, gameplay unfolds through three distinct modes that represent different paces and focuses of adventure. These modes—Encounter, Exploration, and Downtime—allow you to experience the antediluvian world at varying levels of detail, from moment-to-moment combat against Nephilim raiders to weeks-long journeys across flood-ravaged plains to months of spiritual reflection in hidden Sethite enclaves. Understanding when and how to transition between these modes is essential for both players and Game Masters.  
Unlike medieval fantasy settings where gameplay might focus on dungeon crawling or courtly intrigue, Nephilim Wars emphasizes survival in a world dominated by titanic foes, moral choices with eternal consequences, and the looming certainty of divine judgment. Every action you take—from striking a blow against a Rephaim warrior to sharing bread with a starving Wanderer—carries weight in a world where the Flood approaches and every soul is being weighed.  
This chapter adapts the Pathfinder 2e core gameplay systems to the bronze-age reality of the antediluvian world. While the fundamental mechanics remain familiar to PF2e veterans, each system has been infused with setting-specific flavor, Soul Economy integration, and bronze-age examples. Whether you are a seasoned Pathfinder player or new to the system, this chapter provides everything you need to bring the age before the Flood to life at your gaming table.  
The Three-Action Economy  
Core Principles  
Pathfinder 2e's three-action economy represents the fundamental rhythm of gameplay. During your turn in Encounter Mode, you have three actions to spend on various activities—attacking, moving, using skills, casting spells, or interacting with the environment. This system creates tactical depth while maintaining clarity about what characters can accomplish in a given moment.  
In the antediluvian world, these actions represent more than mere mechanical choices—they embody the desperate struggle for survival against overwhelming odds. A Warrior spending all three actions to Strike a Nephilim warlord represents a desperate, all-out assault against a titanic foe. A Hunter using one action to Hide, one to Move, and one to Strike represents the calculated precision of wilderness warfare. A Priest spending actions to cast divine miracles while maintaining defensive positioning represents the delicate balance between offense and protection in a world where every ally matters.  
Action Types and Antediluvian Examples  
Single Actions (\[A\]): The most common type of action, representing discrete activities that take a few seconds to complete.  
Examples:  
Strike: Kedar the Warrior swings his bronze greatsword at a Nephilim scout's leg, aiming for the vulnerable joint where giant hide meets scaled armor.  
Move: Tamar the Hunter darts between fallen logs to reposition herself for a better shot with her composite longbow.  
Cast a Spell: Keturah the Magi gestures with her bronze-studded fingers, summoning Azazel's Flame to engulf a group of Rephaim thralls.  
Hide: Rahab the Gibbor slips behind a massive stone block, using the oversized environment to conceal her hybrid form from patrolling Anakim guards.  
Interact: Enoch the Priest reaches into his pouch to retrieve a clay vial of blessed water while maintaining his defensive stance.  
Two-Action Activities (\[AA\]): More complex activities requiring greater time and focus.  
Examples:  
Cast a Spell (complex): Keturah channels Baraqel's Star Reading, spending two actions to trace celestial patterns in the air and gain insight into her enemies' weaknesses.  
Shove: Kedar braces himself against the ground and uses both hands on his greatsword's pommel to shove a wounded Nephilim warrior toward a cliff edge.  
Search: Tamar spends two actions carefully examining the ground around a Nephilim campsite, looking for tracks that might reveal their numbers and direction.  
Demoralize: Rahab lets out a guttural roar that echoes with her giant heritage, attempting to frighten a group of human thralls into surrendering.  
Three-Action Activities (\[AAA\]): The most demanding activities, representing significant efforts or complex maneuvers.  
Examples:  
Cast a Spell (powerful): Keturah performs the full ritual of Semyaza's Binding, spending three actions to weave chains of shadow around a disembodied Refa'im spirit.  
Fight Defensively: Kedar adopts a wide-legged stance, planting his feet firmly and raising his shield to protect nearby allies while remaining ready to strike.  
Treat Wounds: Enoch kneels beside a wounded Wanderer, spending three actions to clean the wound with blessed water, apply healing herbs, and speak words of comfort.  
Long Rest (downtime): The party finds shelter in a cave and spends three actions (representing hours) resting, tending wounds, and preparing for the next day's journey.  
Free Actions and Reactions  
Free Actions: Activities that require minimal time or effort, often triggered by other events.  
Examples:  
Speak: Enoch calls out a warning to his allies: "The Nephilim's shadow falls upon us—prepare yourselves\!"  
Drop an Item: Tamar releases her bowstring after firing, letting the weapon hang from its strap as she draws a bronze dagger for close combat.  
Stand Up: Rahab rises from a crouch after using the environment for cover, her muscles coiling with ancestral strength.  
Reactions: Immediate responses to specific triggers, representing split-second decisions in the heat of battle.  
Examples:  
Raise a Shield: Kedar snaps his bronze-reinforced shield into position as a Nephilim warlord's massive spear hurtles toward him.  
Attack of Opportunity: Tamar's arrow flies as a Rephaim warrior attempts to move past her position, striking at the giant's exposed neck.  
Divine Shield: Enoch extends his hand, calling upon the Most High to interpose divine energy between an ally and a corrupted sorcerer's spell.  
Action Economy and the Soul Economy  
The three-action economy interacts with the Soul Economy in meaningful ways. Certain actions may generate or consume Righteousness Points (RP) or Corruption Points (CP), creating strategic considerations beyond mere tactical advantage.  
RP-Generating Actions:  
Protecting the Innocent: Using the Aid action to grant an ally a bonus against a Nephilim attack may generate 1 RP if the ally is significantly weaker or more vulnerable.  
Showing Mercy: Using the Demoralize action to convince enemies to surrender rather than killing them may generate 1 RP on success.  
Keeping Covenants: Spending actions to fulfill a sacred oath (such as protecting a Sethite elder) may generate RP based on the difficulty and importance of the task.  
CP-Generating Actions:  
Using Forbidden Magic: Casting spells from Watcher traditions consumes actions and generates CP based on the spell's level and harmful nature.  
Cruel Violence: Using multiple Strike actions to brutally execute surrendered enemies may generate 1 CP per excessive attack.  
Breaking Oaths: Using actions to betray a covenant or abandon allies in danger may generate CP based on the severity of the betrayal.  
RP-Spending Actions:  
Divine Intervention: Spending 1 RP as a free action to reroll a failed saving throw against a Nephilim's fear aura.  
Righteous Strike: Spending 1 RP as part of a Strike action to deal additional radiant damage to a corrupted target.  
Healing Touch: Spending RP as part of the Treat Wounds action to restore additional Hit Points or remove conditions.  
The integration of these systems creates meaningful choices: Do you spend an extra action to show mercy and gain RP, or do you use that action for a killing blow that might generate CP but ensure immediate safety? Do you conserve RP for critical moments, or do you spend it freely to maximize your effectiveness in every encounter?  
Encounter Mode  
Definition and Purpose  
Encounter Mode represents the most detailed and tactical level of gameplay, used when precise timing and positioning matter—typically during combat, but also during tense social interactions, complex skill challenges, or dangerous environmental hazards. In Encounter Mode, time is measured in rounds (6 seconds each), each character takes individual turns in initiative order, and every action has immediate consequences.  
In the antediluvian world, Encounter Mode is most commonly triggered by:  
Combat with Giants: Facing Nephilim warriors, Rephaim champions, or other titanic foes where positioning and timing are critical  
Ambushes and Raids: Defending against or executing surprise attacks in wilderness or urban environments  
Spiritual Confrontations: Battling disembodied Refa'im spirits or resisting possession attempts  
Dangerous Rituals: Performing or interrupting Watcher-taught ceremonies that risk corruption or divine judgment  
Environmental Hazards: Navigating flood waters, earthquake zones, or other catastrophic events  
Initiative and Turn Order  
Determining Initiative: At the start of an encounter, each participant rolls a d20 and adds their Dexterity modifier and any other relevant bonuses (such as from feats or items). The result determines their position in the initiative order, from highest to lowest.  
Soul Economy Modifier: Corruption Points impose a penalty on initiative rolls, representing the sluggish spirit of a corrupted soul. Subtract your total CP from your initiative modifier. This penalty can be significant for characters who have embraced forbidden knowledge or committed corrupt acts.  
Example: Keturah the Magi has 4 CP from frequent use of forbidden magic. Her normal initiative modifier is \+5 (from her Dexterity), but with the CP penalty, her effective initiative modifier is \+1. In combat against a Nephilim patrol, she acts much later than she normally would, her corrupted soul slowing her reactions.  
Tiebreakers: If two or more participants roll the same initiative result, the character with the higher Dexterity modifier acts first. If Dexterity modifiers are also tied, the GM decides the order (typically favoring player characters over NPCs, or more significant NPCs over minions).  
Combat Sequence  
Step 1: Determine Surprise  
Before rolling initiative, the GM determines if any participants are surprised. A character is surprised if they fail to notice a threat that has successfully Hidden or used Stealth to approach undetected.  
Antediluvian Example: A Nephilim scout has been tracking the party for hours, using the oversized environment to conceal its approach. The GM calls for Stealth checks opposed by the party's Perception checks. Tamar the Hunter succeeds at her Perception check and notices the scout's massive shadow moving between trees, but Kedar the Warrior fails and remains unaware. When combat begins, Kedar is surprised for the first round, unable to act while the Nephilim scout attacks.  
Step 2: Roll Initiative  
All participants roll initiative simultaneously. The GM records the results and establishes the turn order.  
Step 3: Take Turns  
Each participant takes their turn in initiative order, spending up to three actions and any number of free actions. Reactions can be used at any time during the round, even on other participants' turns.  
Step 4: Repeat  
After all participants have taken their turns, the round ends and a new round begins. This continues until the encounter concludes.  
Movement in Combat  
Speed and Movement Actions: Your Speed determines how far you can move in a single action. Most characters have a Speed of 25 feet, though this varies by ancestry and equipment.  
Antediluvian Considerations:  
Large Creatures: Nephilim and other Large ancestries typically have 10-foot reach and occupy 10-foot squares, affecting movement and positioning in combat.  
Difficult Terrain: Flood plains, muddy ground, rocky canyons, and debris-filled ruins are common in the antediluvian world. Moving through difficult terrain costs 10 feet of movement per 5 feet traveled.  
Environmental Hazards: Rising waters, falling rocks, and unstable ground may require Acrobatics or Athletics checks to navigate safely during movement.  
Movement Options:  
Step: Move 5 feet without triggering reactions. Useful for minor repositioning without exposing yourself to opportunity attacks.  
Stride: Move up to your Speed. The most common movement action.  
Climb: Move vertically or across difficult surfaces. Requires an Athletics check if the surface is particularly challenging (DC varies by surface).  
Swim: Move through water. Requires an Athletics check if the water is turbulent or deep (DC varies by conditions).  
Tumble Through: Move through an enemy's space. Requires an Acrobatics check (DC \= 15 \+ enemy's level). Failure means you don't move and trigger an Attack of Opportunity.  
Example: Tamar needs to reach a higher vantage point to get a clear shot at a Nephilim warlord. She uses one action to Stride 25 feet to the base of a rocky outcrop, then spends her second action to Climb. The GM sets the DC at 18 for the slippery, moss-covered rocks. Tamar rolls a 14 on her Athletics check, plus her \+7 modifier, for a total of 21—success\! She reaches the top and uses her third action to draw her composite longbow.  
Attacks and Damage  
Making an Attack:  
Choose a target within range  
Roll a d20 and add your attack modifier (proficiency bonus \+ ability modifier \+ other bonuses)  
Compare the result to the target's AC  
On a success, roll damage; on a critical success (natural 20 or meeting DC by 10+), roll damage twice  
Attack Modifiers in the Antediluvian World:  
Proficiency Bonus: Based on your proficiency rank with the weapon (Trained \+3, Expert \+5, Master \+7, Legendary \+9 at level 20\)  
Ability Modifier: Strength for melee weapons, Dexterity for ranged weapons and finesse weapons  
Magic/Quality Bonus: From masterwork or magical weapons (+1 to \+3)  
Circumstance Bonuses: From flanking, high ground, or tactical advantages  
Status Bonuses: From spells, abilities, or conditions  
Damage Types and Antediluvian Relevance:  
Bludgeoning: Hammers, clubs, fists—effective against armored foes and giant hide  
Piercing: Spears, arrows, daggers—good for finding gaps in armor or targeting vulnerable spots  
Slashing: Swords, axes—versatile damage type, effective against most targets  
Fire: Azazel's warfare magic, divine judgment—effective against corrupted beings and wooden structures  
Radiant: Divine miracles, angelic power—particularly effective against undead and corrupted beings  
Negative: Necromancy, demonic power—effective against living creatures, especially the righteous  
Force: Pure magical energy—bypasses most resistances, useful against incorporeal spirits  
Example: Kedar faces a Nephilim warrior wearing bronze scale armor. He swings his bronze greatsword (1d12 slashing damage) with a \+14 attack modifier. The Nephilim's AC is 22\. Kedar rolls a 16 on the d20, for a total of 30—success\! He rolls 1d12+6 for damage, getting an 8 for a total of 14 slashing damage. The Nephilim roars in pain but remains standing, its massive frame absorbing the blow that would have felled a human opponent.  
Special Combat Actions  
Fight Defensively: Spend all three actions to gain a \+2 circumstance bonus to AC until the start of your next turn. Useful when protecting allies or when you need to survive a powerful enemy's attack.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar stands between a wounded Sethite elder and an advancing Anakim noble. He spends all three actions fighting defensively, planting his feet and raising his shield. The Anakim's massive bronze axe swings down, but Kedar's defensive stance and shield turn aside the blow that would have crushed the elder.  
Shove: Attempt to push a creature away from you. Make an Athletics check opposed by the target's Athletics or Acrobatics check. On success, push the target 5 feet; on critical success, push 10 feet.  
Antediluvian Example: Rahab faces a Rephaim warrior in a narrow canyon. She uses her Giant's Grip feat to wield her bronze greatsword one-handed, freeing her other hand to Shove. She makes an Athletics check (DC 22\) and succeeds, pushing the Rephaim back into a deep crevice where it becomes trapped.  
Trip: Attempt to knock a creature prone. Make an Athletics or Acrobatics check opposed by the target's Acrobatics or Athletics check. On success, the target falls prone; on critical success, they also take 1d6 bludgeoning damage.  
Antediluvian Example: Tamar uses her longbow to target a Nephilim scout's legs. She spends two actions to make a Trip attempt with her bow's butt end. The GM sets the DC at 24 for the massive giant. Tamar rolls a 19 on her Acrobatics check, plus her \+8 modifier, for a total of 27—success\! The Nephilim stumbles and falls to one knee, its massive form crashing against the canyon wall.  
Grapple: Attempt to grab and restrain a creature. Make an Athletics check opposed by the target's Athletics or Acrobatics check. On success, the target is grabbed; on critical success, they are restrained.  
Antediluvian Example: An Anakim noble swings its heavy chains at Kedar. Kedar uses his shield to block the blow, then grabs one of the chains with his free hand. He makes an Athletics check (DC 26\) and succeeds, pulling the Anakim off balance and wrapping the chains around its own limbs.  
Environmental Interactions  
The antediluvian world is filled with environmental features that can be used strategically in combat:  
Natural Features:  
Fallen Trees: Provide cover (+2 circumstance bonus to AC) and can be used as improvised weapons or barriers  
Rock Formations: Offer high ground (+1 circumstance bonus to attack rolls) and can be dislodged to create hazards  
River Banks: Create difficult terrain and can be used to force enemies into water where they may struggle  
Cave Entrances: Provide choke points where smaller characters can defend against larger foes  
Structural Features:  
Nephilim Fortifications: Massive walls, gates, and towers that can be used for cover or as platforms for ranged attacks  
Cainite Buildings: Bronze-reinforced structures that can be collapsed or used for ambushes  
Sethite Altars: Sacred sites that may provide divine bonuses or protection against corruption  
Watcher Ruins: Ancient structures imbued with forbidden power that may grant advantages or risks  
Example: The party is ambushed by a Nephilim patrol in a narrow canyon. Tamar uses her knowledge of the terrain to identify a precariously balanced boulder above the path. She spends one action to Stride into position, one action to use Athletics to push the boulder (DC 20), and one action to fire an arrow at a Nephilim scout to draw its attention. The boulder crashes down, creating difficult terrain and separating the Nephilim forces, allowing the party to deal with them in smaller groups.  
Combat and the Soul Economy  
Every combat encounter in Nephilim Wars carries spiritual weight. The choices you make in battle—whom you fight, how you fight, and why you fight—affect your soul's condition.  
Gaining RP in Combat:  
Protecting the Innocent: Using your actions to defend non-combatants, wounded allies, or righteous NPCs  
Showing Mercy: Accepting surrenders, sparing defeated enemies who pose no threat, or using non-lethal force when possible  
Fighting for Righteous Causes: Battling corrupted beings, defending sacred sites, or protecting the righteous line  
Resisting Corruption: Refusing to use forbidden magic or cruel tactics even when they would be tactically advantageous  
Gaining CP in Combat:  
Using Forbidden Magic: Casting spells from Watcher traditions, especially harmful ones  
Cruel Violence: Executing surrendered enemies, torturing prisoners, or using excessive force beyond what is necessary  
Fighting for Corrupt Causes: Serving Nephilim overlords, attacking righteous communities, or protecting corrupted beings  
Breaking Sacred Oaths: Betraying allies, abandoning those you've sworn to protect, or violating covenants during combat  
Combat Examples:  
Scenario 1: The party faces a group of Nephilim thralls—humans forced to serve giant masters. Kedar could kill them all with efficient brutality, but instead he uses non-lethal attacks and attempts to free them from their bonds. For showing mercy and seeking to liberate rather than destroy, he gains 1 RP.  
Scenario 2: Keturah faces a Sethite elder who opposes her use of forbidden magic. She could use non-lethal force or attempt to reason with the elder, but instead she casts Azazel's Flame, severely burning the righteous NPC. For using forbidden magic against a righteous target and causing unnecessary harm, she gains 2 CP.  
Scenario 3: Rahab battles a Rephaim champion that has been terrorizing a Wanderer tribe. She fights with all her strength, using her giant heritage to match the Rephaim's power, but she does so to protect the innocent and uphold justice. For fighting for a righteous cause without unnecessary cruelty, she gains 1 RP.  
Exploration Mode  
Definition and Purpose  
Exploration Mode represents the middle level of gameplay detail, used when the party is traveling through the wilderness, exploring ruins, searching for resources, or engaging in activities that take minutes to hours rather than seconds. In Exploration Mode, time is measured in minutes or hours, characters act simultaneously rather than taking individual turns, and the focus shifts from tactical precision to strategic decision-making and resource management.  
In the antediluvian world, Exploration Mode is essential for:  
Wilderness Travel: Journeying across flood plains, deserts, mountains, or forests between settlements  
Scouting and Reconnaissance: Gathering information about Nephilim territories, Cainite cities, or Sethite enclaves  
Resource Gathering: Hunting, foraging, mining, or collecting materials needed for survival or crafting  
Environmental Navigation: Dealing with rising waters, earthquakes, storms, or other signs of the approaching Flood  
Ruins Exploration: Investigating ancient Watcher sites, abandoned settlements, or sacred locations  
Exploration Activities  
During Exploration Mode, each character typically performs one exploration activity per minute (or per hour, depending on the pace). These activities represent ongoing tasks that characters perform while traveling or exploring.  
Common Exploration Activities:  
Avoid Notice: Attempt to move stealthily through dangerous territory. Make a Stealth check (DC varies by environment and threats). Success means you avoid detection; failure may trigger an encounter.  
Antediluvian Example: The party must cross a Nephilim-patrolled plain to reach a hidden Sethite enclave. Tamar leads the way, using her Survival and Stealth skills to find paths that minimize exposure. The GM sets the DC at 18 for the open terrain. Tamar rolls a 15 on her Stealth check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 24—success\! The party crosses the plain undetected, avoiding a potentially deadly confrontation.  
Detect Magic: Constantly scan for magical auras while exploring. Make an Occultism or Religion check (DC varies by magic strength and concealment). Success reveals the presence and general nature of magical effects.  
Antediluvian Example: The party explores the ruins of an ancient Watcher teaching site. Keturah uses her Detect Magic activity to scan for residual forbidden energy. The GM sets the DC at 22 for the subtle, ancient magic. Keturah rolls a 12 on her Occultism check, plus her \+8 modifier, for a total of 20—failure. She doesn't detect the corrupted aura that permeates the ruins, and the party unknowingly absorbs spiritual contamination (each member gains 1 CP).  
Follow the Expert: Assist another character in their exploration activity. Make a skill check using the same skill as the lead character. Success grants the lead character a \+1 circumstance bonus; critical success grants \+2.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar leads the party through a treacherous mountain pass, using his Survival skill to find safe paths. Enoch follows the expert, using his own Survival skill to help identify hazards. Kedar's Survival check receives a \+1 circumstance bonus from Enoch's assistance, helping the party avoid a dangerous avalanche zone.  
Hustle: Move at double your normal overland speed, but take a \-2 penalty to all skill checks during this time. Useful when you need to cover ground quickly but risky in dangerous territory.  
Antediluvian Example: The party learns that a Nephilim warband is marching toward a Wanderer camp they recently left. They decide to hustle back to warn the camp, doubling their travel speed from 24 miles per day to 48 miles per day. However, their \-2 penalty to Perception checks means they fail to notice a Rephaim scout tracking them, leading to an ambush later that evening.  
Investigate: Search an area thoroughly for clues, hidden features, or valuable items. Make a Perception or relevant skill check (DC varies by what you're looking for). Success reveals information or items; critical success reveals additional details.  
Antediluvian Example: The party explores a cave system rumored to contain Horim settlements. Tamar investigates the cave walls, looking for signs of habitation. The GM sets the DC at 20 for subtle Horim markings. Tamar rolls a 19 on her Perception check, plus her \+7 modifier, for a total of 26—critical success\! She not only finds evidence of Horim presence but also discovers a hidden passage leading to an underground chamber containing ancient stoneworking tools.  
Repair: Fix damaged equipment while traveling. Make a Crafting check (DC \= 15 \+ item's level). Success restores the item to functional condition; critical success restores it to full Hit Points.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar's bronze scale armor was damaged in a recent battle with Nephilim scouts. During a rest break, he spends an hour repairing the armor. The GM sets the DC at 17 for the bronze scale armor. Kedar rolls a 14 on his Crafting check, plus his \+5 modifier, for a total of 19—success\! His armor is restored to functional condition, though it still bears visible scars from the battle.  
Scout: Move ahead of the party to gather information about upcoming terrain, threats, or opportunities. Make a Survival check (DC varies by terrain complexity). Success reveals information about the path ahead; critical success reveals hidden dangers or opportunities.  
Antediluvian Example: Tamar scouts ahead of the party as they approach a flooded river valley. The GM sets the DC at 19 for the complex, changing terrain. Tamar rolls a 16 on her Survival check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 25—critical success\! She not only finds a safe ford across the river but also spots a Nephilim patrol camped downstream, allowing the party to plan their crossing to avoid detection.  
Search: Look for specific items, tracks, or features in an area. Make a Perception or relevant skill check (DC varies by what you're searching for). Success finds the target; critical success finds it quickly or reveals additional information.  
Antediluvian Example: The party searches a battlefield where a Sethite community was attacked by Nephilim raiders. Enoch searches for survivors or bodies to give proper burial. The GM sets the DC at 21 for finding signs of life in the carnage. Enoch rolls a 13 on his Medicine check, plus his \+8 modifier, for a total of 21—success\! He finds a wounded Sethite child hiding beneath the bodies of her parents, still alive but in critical condition.  
Subsist: Find food and water for the party while traveling. Make a Survival check (DC varies by environment fertility). Success provides enough resources for the day; critical success provides extra or particularly nutritious resources.  
Antediluvian Example: The party travels through a drought-stricken region where water is scarce. Tamar attempts to subsist, using her Survival skill to find water sources. The GM sets the DC at 23 for the arid environment. Tamar rolls a 17 on her Survival check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 26—critical success\! She not only finds a hidden spring but also identifies edible plants that provide extra nourishment, granting the party a \+1 circumstance bonus to saving throws against fatigue for the next day.  
Exploration Pace  
The pace at which the party travels affects both their speed and their ability to notice details:  
Quick Pace: Move at 1.5 times normal speed, but take a \-2 penalty to all exploration activity checks. Useful when time is critical or the path is well-known and safe.  
Normal Pace: Move at standard speed with no penalties or bonuses to exploration activities. The default pace for most travel.  
Slow Pace: Move at half normal speed, but gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to all exploration activity checks. Useful when navigating dangerous or unfamiliar terrain, or when thoroughness is more important than speed.  
Antediluvian Example: The party must cross a region known for Nephilim patrols and corrupted wildlife. They decide to travel at a slow pace, accepting the reduced speed in exchange for the \+1 bonus to their Stealth and Perception checks. This cautious approach allows them to avoid several potential encounters, though it adds two days to their journey.  
Environmental Hazards in Exploration  
The antediluvian world is filled with environmental dangers that can threaten the party during exploration:  
Flood Waters: Rising waters can block paths, create dangerous currents, or sweep away unprepared travelers. Swimming through flood waters requires Athletics checks (DC varies by water speed and depth). Failure means being swept away or drowning.  
Earthquakes: The approaching Flood causes increasing seismic activity. Earthquakes can create chasms, collapse structures, or trigger landslides. Reflex saves may be required to avoid falling debris or being thrown to the ground.  
Storms: Violent storms with high winds, lightning, and torrential rain become more frequent as judgment approaches. Storms can reduce visibility, make travel dangerous, or attract corrupted beings that thrive in chaos.  
Corrupted Lands: Areas tainted by Watcher influence or Nephilim corruption may have twisted vegetation, poisoned water, or spiritual contamination. Spending time in corrupted lands may require saving throws against gaining CP or suffering other effects.  
Extreme Temperatures: Desert heat, mountain cold, or unnatural weather patterns can threaten survival. Fortitude saves may be required to avoid exhaustion, dehydration, or exposure.  
Example: The party travels through a region that was once fertile but has been corrupted by a bound Watcher's influence. The GM describes twisted trees that bleed black sap, water sources that smell of decay, and an oppressive atmosphere that weighs on the soul. Each hour spent in this area requires a DC 15 Will save. Failure means gaining 1 CP from spiritual contamination; critical failure means gaining 2 CP and suffering the sickened condition for 1 hour.  
Exploration and the Soul Economy  
Exploration activities provide numerous opportunities to gain or lose RP and CP:  
Gaining RP through Exploration:  
Guiding the Lost: Using Survival or Society skills to lead refugees to safety  
Preserving Knowledge: Using Investigation or Society skills to recover and protect ancient texts or sacred artifacts  
Healing the Land: Using Nature or Medicine skills to restore corrupted areas or heal wounded wildlife  
Protecting the Weak: Using Scout or Stealth skills to avoid leading threats to vulnerable communities  
Gaining CP through Exploration:  
Desecrating Sacred Sites: Using Investigation or Thievery skills to loot or defile righteous locations  
Exploiting the Vulnerable: Using Subsist or Survival skills to take resources needed by others without compensation  
Studying Forbidden Knowledge: Using Occultism or Society skills to research Watcher texts or corrupted practices  
Leading Threats to Innocents: Using Scout or Survival skills to deliberately guide dangers toward settlements  
Exploration Examples:  
Scenario 1: The party discovers a group of Sethite refugees fleeing a Nephilim raid. Tamar uses her Survival skill to guide them to a hidden valley where they can establish a temporary settlement. For protecting the innocent and using her skills for righteous purposes, Tamar gains 1 RP.  
Scenario 2: Keturah finds an ancient tablet containing forbidden Watcher teachings. Despite warnings from Enoch, she spends hours studying the text, using her Occultism skill to decipher its secrets. For deliberately seeking corrupted knowledge, she gains 2 CP, and the GM requires a flat check (DC 13\) to avoid gaining an additional CP from the study itself.  
Scenario 3: The party comes across a corrupted spring that has poisoned a Wanderer tribe's water source. Enoch uses his Religion skill to perform a purification ritual, while Kedar uses his Crafting skill to build a filtration system from available materials. For working together to heal the land and protect the innocent, both characters gain 1 RP.  
Downtime Mode  
Definition and Purpose  
Downtime Mode represents the least detailed level of gameplay, used when the party is resting between adventures, pursuing long-term goals, or engaging in activities that take days, weeks, or months. In Downtime Mode, time is measured in days or longer periods, characters pursue individual or group projects, and the focus shifts from immediate survival to character development, community building, and preparation for future challenges.  
In the antediluvian world, Downtime Mode is crucial for:  
Spiritual Development: Prayer, meditation, study of sacred texts, and righteous service  
Crafting and Preparation: Creating weapons, armor, tools, and supplies needed for survival  
Community Building: Establishing or strengthening relationships with settlements, tribes, or factions  
Knowledge Preservation: Recording histories, prophecies, and wisdom for future generations  
Rest and Recovery: Healing wounds, removing corruption, and preparing for the challenges ahead  
Downtime Activities  
During Downtime Mode, each character typically performs one downtime activity per day (or per week/month for longer activities). These activities represent focused efforts toward specific goals.  
Common Downtime Activities:  
Crafting: Create items, repair equipment, or improve existing gear. The time required depends on the item's cost and complexity. Success creates the item; critical success creates a higher-quality item or reduces the time required.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar spends a week crafting a new bronze greatsword to replace his damaged weapon. The sword costs 50 gp, so he needs to earn 50 gp worth of progress. With his Crafting skill (+7) and blacksmith's tools, he can make 10 gp of progress per day. After 5 days of work (and spending 25 gp on materials), he completes the sword. The GM allows a critical success on his final Crafting check to add a special property—the sword deals an additional 1 damage against creatures larger than Medium, representing Kedar's specialized training against giants.  
Earn Income: Work at a job or trade to earn money or resources. Make a skill check related to your profession (DC varies by job difficulty and location). Success earns income; critical success earns extra income or additional benefits.  
Antediluvian Example: Tamar spends a month working as a guide for a Cainite merchant caravan traveling between cities. She uses her Survival skill (+9) to navigate safe routes and avoid Nephilim territories. The GM sets the DC at 20 for the dangerous journey. Tamar rolls a 15 on her Survival check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 24—success\! She earns 50 gp for the month, plus the merchant's gratitude, which grants her a \+1 circumstance bonus to Diplomacy checks with Cainite merchants in the future.  
Practice: Train to improve a skill or learn a new ability. Make a skill check related to what you're practicing (DC varies by difficulty). Success improves your proficiency or grants a new ability; critical success provides additional benefits.  
Antediluvian Example: Rahab spends two weeks practicing her Giant's Grip technique, working with oversized weapons and training against larger opponents. She uses her Athletics skill (+8) to develop the strength and coordination needed. The GM sets the DC at 22 for mastering this advanced technique. Rahab rolls a 16 on her Athletics check, plus her \+8 modifier, for a total of 24—success\! She gains the Improved Giant's Grip feat, allowing her to wield two-handed weapons in one hand without the normal \-2 penalty.  
Recover: Rest and heal from injuries, diseases, or conditions. Make a Medicine or flat check (DC varies by condition severity). Success removes the condition or restores Hit Points; critical success provides additional healing or removes multiple conditions.  
Antediluvian Example: Enoch spends a week in prayer and meditation at a Sethite enclave, seeking to heal the spiritual wounds from recent battles. He uses his Religion skill (+10) to guide his recovery. The GM sets the DC at 18 for overcoming the trauma of facing corrupted beings. Enoch rolls a 12 on his Religion check, plus his \+10 modifier, for a total of 22—success\! He removes the frightened condition that had been affecting him since encountering a powerful Refa'im spirit, and he gains 1 RP from the righteous meditation.  
Research: Study texts, consult experts, or investigate mysteries to gain knowledge. Make a skill check related to your research (DC varies by information obscurity). Success reveals information; critical success reveals additional details or grants a bonus.  
Antediluvian Example: Keturah spends a month researching forbidden Watcher texts in a hidden Sorcerer Clan library. She uses her Occultism skill (+8) to decipher ancient writings. The GM sets the DC at 25 for the dangerous, corrupted knowledge. Keturah rolls a 14 on her Occultism check, plus her \+8 modifier, for a total of 22—failure. She doesn't gain the knowledge she sought, and the GM requires a flat check (DC 15\) for corruption risk. Keturah fails the flat check and gains 1 CP from exposure to the corrupted texts.  
Train Animal: Teach an animal new tricks, improve its abilities, or strengthen your bond. Make a Nature check (DC varies by animal intelligence and training complexity). Success trains the animal; critical success provides additional benefits.  
Antediluvian Example: Tamar spends two weeks training her wolf companion, Asher, to better assist in combat against giants. She uses her Nature skill (+9) to teach new commands. The GM sets the DC at 20 for advanced combat training. Tamar rolls a 17 on her Nature check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 26—critical success\! Asher learns to attack the legs of Large or larger creatures, imposing a \-1 penalty on their attack rolls for 1 round after a successful Strike.  
Work: Perform labor or services for a community or employer. Make a skill check related to your work (DC varies by job difficulty). Success earns wages or favors; critical success earns extra benefits or recognition.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar spends a month working as a guard for a Wanderer tribe, protecting them from predators and raiders. He uses his Intimidation skill (+7) to deter potential threats. The GM sets the DC at 18 for maintaining security in dangerous territory. Kedar rolls a 13 on his Intimidation check, plus his \+7 modifier, for a total of 20—success\! He earns 30 gp for the month, plus the tribe's gratitude, which grants him a \+1 circumstance bonus to Diplomacy checks with Wanderer tribes in the future.  
Special Downtime Activities: Soul Economy Development  
The antediluvian world offers unique downtime activities focused on spiritual development and the Soul Economy:  
Repentance: Spend time in prayer, meditation, and righteous service to remove Corruption Points. Make a Will save (DC \= 15 \+ your current CP). Success removes 1 CP; critical success removes 2 CP. This activity takes one week per attempt.  
Example: Keturah has accumulated 5 CP from frequent use of forbidden magic. She spends a week in a Sethite enclave, confessing her corruption and seeking forgiveness. The GM sets the DC at 20 (15 \+ 5 CP). Keturah rolls a 14 on her Will save, plus her \+3 modifier, for a total of 17—failure. She doesn't remove any CP, but the GM allows her to try again the following week, representing her persistent efforts at redemption.  
Righteous Service: Perform acts of service for a righteous community or cause. Make a skill check related to your service (DC varies by service difficulty). Success gains 1 RP; critical success gains 2 RP. This activity takes one week per attempt.  
Example: Enoch spends a week teaching sacred texts to young Sethites in a hidden enclave. He uses his Religion skill (+10) to instruct effectively. The GM sets the DC at 18 for meaningful spiritual education. Enoch rolls a 15 on his Religion check, plus his \+10 modifier, for a total of 25—critical success\! He gains 2 RP from the meaningful service, and the students gain a \+1 circumstance bonus to Will saves against corruption for the next month.  
Forbidden Study: Deliberately study corrupted texts or practices to gain power. Make an Occultism check (DC varies by knowledge danger). Success gains a temporary benefit or ability; failure gains 1 CP; critical failure gains 2 CP and suffers a corruption effect.  
Example: Keturah seeks to learn a powerful forbidden spell to combat an approaching Nephilim warlord. She spends a week studying a dangerous Watcher text. The GM sets the DC at 24 for the high-risk knowledge. Keturah rolls a 16 on her Occultism check, plus her \+8 modifier, for a total of 24—success\! She learns the spell but must make a flat check (DC 17\) for corruption risk. She fails and gains 1 CP, but she gains the ability to cast the spell once before needing to rest.  
Pilgrimage: Travel to a sacred site or location of spiritual significance. Make a Survival check (DC varies by journey difficulty). Success grants spiritual benefits; critical success grants additional benefits. This activity takes one week per journey segment.  
Example: Enoch undertakes a pilgrimage to Mount Hermon, the site where the 200 Watchers descended. The journey takes three weeks through dangerous territory. For each week, he makes a Survival check (DC 20). He succeeds on all three checks, reaching the mountain without incident. At the summit, he spends a day in prayer and receives a vision of the coming Flood, gaining 2 RP and a \+1 circumstance bonus to saves against fear effects for the next month.  
Downtime Projects  
Some goals require extended downtime efforts spanning weeks or months:  
Establishing a Refuge: Create a safe haven for refugees, righteous communities, or resistance groups. Requires multiple Crafting, Society, and Survival checks over several months. Success creates a functional refuge; critical success creates a fortified sanctuary with special benefits.  
Building a Resistance Network: Establish connections with multiple communities to coordinate resistance against Nephilim oppression. Requires multiple Diplomacy, Society, and Stealth checks over several months. Success creates a functional network; critical success creates an extensive network with special capabilities.  
Preserving Knowledge: Compile and protect ancient texts, prophecies, and wisdom for future generations. Requires multiple Society, Religion, and Crafting checks over several months. Success creates a protected archive; critical success creates a divinely protected repository that resists corruption and destruction.  
Forging a Legendary Weapon: Create a weapon of mythic power capable of slaying the mightiest Nephilim. Requires multiple Crafting, Religion, and special material checks over several months. Success creates a powerful weapon; critical success creates an artifact with unique properties.  
Example: The party decides to establish a refuge for Sethite refugees fleeing Nephilim persecution. They spend three months working together:  
Kedar leads construction efforts (Crafting checks)  
Tamar scouts the location and establishes defensive positions (Survival checks)  
Enoch blesses the site and establishes spiritual practices (Religion checks)  
Keturah reluctantly helps with security, though her presence risks corruption (Occultism checks with CP risk)  
After three months of work and multiple successful checks, they establish "Enoch's Haven," a hidden valley settlement that can house up to 50 refugees. The refuge has fortified walls, hidden entrances, and a sacred grove where divine miracles are enhanced. The party gains 2 RP each for their righteous service, and they establish a base of operations for future adventures.  
Downtime and Character Advancement  
Downtime Mode provides opportunities for character growth beyond mere level advancement:  
Skill Improvements: Characters can use downtime to improve existing skills or learn new ones through practice and study. This represents gradual improvement through experience and training.  
Feat Acquisition: Characters can earn new feats through dedicated training, apprenticeship, or spiritual development during downtime. This represents mastery of specific techniques or abilities.  
Relationship Building: Characters can develop relationships with NPCs, communities, or factions during downtime. These relationships can provide benefits, create obligations, or generate story hooks.  
Spiritual Growth: Characters can use downtime to deepen their connection to the divine (gaining RP) or explore forbidden knowledge (risking CP). This represents the ongoing spiritual journey that defines the antediluvian experience.  
Legacy Building: Characters can use downtime to create lasting impacts on the world—establishing traditions, recording histories, creating artifacts, or founding communities. These legacies may outlast the characters themselves, surviving the Flood to influence future generations.  
Checks and DCs  
Understanding Checks  
In Pathfinder 2e, checks are the fundamental mechanic for resolving uncertain actions. When a character attempts something with a chance of failure, the player rolls a d20 and adds relevant modifiers. The result is compared to a Difficulty Class (DC) to determine success or failure.  
Check Formula:  
d20 \+ Relevant Modifiers ≥ DC \= Success  
Relevant Modifiers Include:  
Ability Modifier: Based on the relevant ability score (STR, DEX, CON, INT, WIS, CHA)  
Proficiency Bonus: Based on proficiency rank (Trained \+3, Expert \+5, Master \+7, Legendary \+9 at level 20\)  
Item Bonus: From weapons, armor, or other equipment (+1 to \+3)  
Circumstance Bonus: From advantageous situations (+1 to \+2)  
Status Bonus: From spells, abilities, or conditions (+1 to \+3)  
Untyped Bonuses: From miscellaneous sources  
Difficulty Classes (DCs)  
DCs represent the difficulty of a task, ranging from trivial to nearly impossible. The standard DC scale in Pathfinder 2e is:  
Task Difficulty  
DC  
Example  
Untrained  
Climbing a rough wall with handholds  
Trained  
Identifying a common plant or animal  
Expert  
Tracking a creature through difficult terrain  
Master  
Deciphering an ancient, corrupted text  
Legendary  
Performing a miracle without divine aid  
Antediluvian DC Adjustments:  
The harsh realities of the antediluvian world may adjust DCs for certain tasks:  
Environmental Hardships: Flood waters, earthquakes, and storms may increase DCs by 2-Resource Scarcity: Lack of proper tools or materials may increase DCs by 2-Spiritual Corruption: Performing righteous acts in corrupted areas may increase DCs by 2-Divine Favor: Performing righteous acts in sacred areas may decrease DCs by 2-Types of Checks  
Attack Rolls: Used to determine if an attack hits its target. Compare the result to the target's AC.  
Skill Checks: Used to resolve skill-based actions. Compare the result to a DC set by the GM based on task difficulty.  
Saving Throws: Used to resist harmful effects. Compare the result to the effect's DC.  
Flat Checks: Used for random chance or simple success/failure. No modifiers are added—just roll the d20 and compare to the DC.  
Recall Knowledge Checks: Used to remember information about creatures, places, or topics. Compare the result to standard DCs (15 for common knowledge, 20 for uncommon, 25 for rare, 30 for secret).  
Critical Successes and Failures  
Critical Success: When you succeed at a check by 10 or more (or roll a natural 20 on an attack roll), you achieve a critical success. This typically provides enhanced effects or additional benefits.  
Antediluvian Example: Tamar attempts to climb a canyon wall to escape a Nephilim patrol. The DC is 20\. She rolls a 15 on her Athletics check, plus her \+9 modifier, for a total of 24—success, but not critical. She reaches the top safely. If she had rolled a 19 (total 28), she would have achieved a critical success, reaching the top quickly and finding a hidden ledge where she could hide from pursuit.  
Critical Failure: When you fail a check by 10 or more (or roll a natural 1 on an attack roll), you suffer a critical failure. This typically results in worse consequences than a normal failure.  
Antediluvian Example: Kedar attempts to intimidate a Nephilim thrall into revealing information. The DC is 18\. He rolls a 2 on his Intimidation check, plus his \+7 modifier, for a total of 9—failure by 9, not quite critical. The thrall refuses to talk. If he had rolled a 1 (total 8), he would have suffered a critical failure, and the thrall might have alerted nearby guards or attacked him in defiance.  
The Soul Economy and Checks  
The Soul Economy can affect checks in several ways:  
RP Bonuses:  
Spending 1 RP to reroll a failed check  
Having high RP granting circumstance bonuses to certain checks (e.g., \+1 to Religion checks at 5+ RP)  
Using RP to fuel abilities that enhance checks (e.g., spending RP to gain a \+2 status bonus to a skill check)  
CP Penalties:  
Having high CP imposing penalties on certain checks (e.g., \-1 to Will saves at 5+ CP)  
Using corrupted abilities that risk gaining CP on failure  
Being in corrupted areas that impose penalties on righteous actions  
Net Righteousness Effects:  
Positive Net Righteousness granting bonuses to divine-related checks  
Negative Net Righteousness granting bonuses to corruption-related checks but penalties to divine checks  
Zero Net Righteousness providing no special bonuses or penalties  
Example: Enoch needs to make a DC 22 Religion check to interpret a prophecy about the coming Flood. He has 7 RP and 2 CP, for a Net Righteousness of \+5 (Blessed tier). His normal Religion modifier is \+10. Because he's in the Blessed tier, he gains a \+1 status bonus to Religion checks. He rolls a 12 on the d20, plus his \+10 modifier, plus his \+1 status bonus, for a total of 23—success\! He correctly interprets the prophecy and gains 1 RP for using his knowledge righteously.  
Conditions  
Standard PF2e Conditions  
Pathfinder 2e includes numerous conditions that represent temporary states affecting characters. These conditions function normally in Nephilim Wars, with the following antediluvian context:  
Blinded: Cannot see. Particularly dangerous in wilderness environments or when facing invisible Refa'im spirits.  
Clumsy: Reduced Dexterity. Represents physical impairment from injury, fatigue, or spiritual burden.  
Concealed: Target has partial cover. Common in wilderness environments, ruins, or when using the oversized environment for cover.  
Confused: Mental impairment. May result from possession attempts, corrupted magic, or spiritual trauma.  
Cover: Physical barrier provides protection. Essential when facing ranged attacks from Nephilim archers or when using environment for defense.  
Dazzled: Vision impaired. May result from divine light, corrupted magic, or environmental effects.  
Deafened: Cannot hear. Dangerous when relying on auditory cues to detect approaching threats.  
Doomed: Marked for death. Represents divine judgment or mortal wounds that cannot be healed.  
Drained: Reduced vitality. May result from blood magic, corrupted rituals, or spiritual exhaustion.  
Encumbered: Carrying too much. Particularly relevant when transporting supplies across long distances or carrying giant-sized equipment.  
Enfeebled: Reduced Strength. Represents physical weakness from injury, starvation, or spiritual corruption.  
Fatigued: Tired and weakened. Common after long journeys, battles, or spiritual struggles.  
Flat-Footed: Unable to react. Dangerous when facing surprise attacks or when caught off-guard by Nephilim ambushes.  
Fleeing: Running away. May result from fear effects, failed morale checks, or strategic retreats.  
Friendly: Allied creature. Important for distinguishing allies from enemies in complex combat situations.  
Grabbed: Restrained by another creature. Common when facing giants with powerful grapples or chain weapons.  
Hidden: Undetected but location known. Essential for stealthy approaches or avoiding detection.  
Immobilized: Cannot move. May result from entanglement, paralysis, or spiritual binding.  
Observed: Creature can see you. Important for stealth and ambush tactics.  
Off-Guard: Vulnerable to attacks. Dangerous when facing skilled opponents or when distracted.  
Paralyzed: Completely immobilized. May result from powerful magic, poison, or spiritual attacks.  
Persistent Damage: Ongoing damage. Common from fire, acid, or corrupted magic.  
Petrified: Turned to stone. May result from powerful magic or divine judgment.  
Prone: Lying on the ground. Dangerous when facing ranged attacks or when unable to defend yourself.  
Quickened: Extra action. Rare and powerful, typically granted by divine intervention or forbidden magic.  
Restrained: Limited movement. Common when captured by Nephilim or bound by corrupted magic.  
Sickened: Physical illness. May result from disease, poison, or spiritual contamination.  
Slowed: Reduced actions. Represents physical or spiritual impairment.  
Stunned: Unable to act. May result from powerful blows, magic, or spiritual attacks.  
Stupefied: Mental impairment. May result from possession, corrupted magic, or spiritual trauma.  
Unconscious: Knocked out. Dangerous when facing enemies who show no mercy.  
Undetected: Completely hidden. Essential for stealthy approaches or avoiding detection.  
Unfriendly: Hostile creature. Important for social interactions and distinguishing threats.  
Unnoticed: Completely undetected. Essential for stealth and ambush tactics.  
Wounded: Injured and vulnerable. Represents physical damage that has not been fully healed.  
New Conditions: Soul Economy Effects  
Nephilim Wars introduces four new conditions that represent the spiritual states of characters in the antediluvian world:  
Blessed  
Level: Variable (1-4)  
Traits: Divine, Positive  
Effect: You are favored by the Most High, your righteousness shining like a beacon in the darkness.  
Mechanical Effects:  
Blessed 1: \+1 status bonus to attack rolls, skill checks, and saving throws  
Blessed 2: \+2 status bonus to attack rolls, skill checks, and saving throws; resistance 2 to all damage from corrupted sources  
Blessed 3: \+3 status bonus to attack rolls, skill checks, and saving throws; resistance 5 to all damage from corrupted sources; immunity to the frightened condition  
Blessed 4: \+4 status bonus to attack rolls, skill checks, and saving throws; resistance 10 to all damage from corrupted sources; immunity to all mental conditions except doomed  
Acquisition: Gain the Blessed condition when your Net Righteousness reaches \+5 or higher. The level equals your Net Righteousness divided by 5 (rounded down, maximum 4).  
Removal: The Blessed condition is removed when your Net Righteousness drops below \+5.  
Antediluvian Flavor: Your presence radiates divine light that is visible to spiritual beings. Corrupted creatures feel discomfort in your presence, and righteous NPCs are drawn to your aura of holiness. You may receive divine visions, prophetic dreams, or guidance in moments of need.  
Example: Enoch has a Net Righteousness of \+12 (15 RP, 3 CP). He gains the Blessed 2 condition (+2 status bonus to all rolls, resistance 2 to corrupted damage). When he enters a corrupted area, the darkness seems to recoil from his presence, and disembodied Refa'im spirits flee from his approach.  
Corrupted  
Level: Variable (1-4)  
Traits: Divine, Negative  
Effect: Your soul is tainted by forbidden knowledge, cruelty, or association with fallen beings.  
Mechanical Effects:  
Corrupted 1: \-1 status penalty to saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities  
Corrupted 2: \-2 status penalty to saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities; vulnerable 2 to radiant damage  
Corrupted 3: \-3 status penalty to saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities; vulnerable 5 to radiant damage; \-1 status penalty to attack rolls against righteous creatures  
Corrupted 4: \-4 status penalty to saving throws against divine effects and righteous abilities; vulnerable 10 to radiant damage; \-2 status penalty to attack rolls against righteous creatures; righteous NPCs may attack you on sight  
Acquisition: Gain the Corrupted condition when your Net Righteousness reaches \-1 or lower. The level equals the absolute value of your Net Righteousness divided by 3 (rounded down, maximum 4).  
Removal: The Corrupted condition is removed when your Net Righteousness rises above 0\. Removing the condition requires genuine repentance, atonement, and often divine intervention.  
Antediluvian Flavor: Your presence radiates spiritual darkness that is visible to those with spiritual sight. Righteous creatures feel discomfort in your presence, and corrupted beings are drawn to your aura of decay. You may hear whispers from disembodied spirits, experience nightmares, or feel the weight of divine judgment approaching.  
Example: Keturah has a Net Righteousness of \-7 (2 RP, 9 CP). She gains the Corrupted 2 condition (-2 penalty to saves against divine effects, vulnerable 2 to radiant damage). When she approaches a Sethite enclave, the guards refuse to let her enter, sensing the corruption that clings to her soul like a shadow.  
Marked for Judgment  
Level: Variable (1-3)  
Traits: Divine, Negative  
Effect: You are specifically targeted by divine judgment for your corruption or rebellion.  
Mechanical Effects:  
Marked for Judgment 1: When you roll a natural 1 on any d20 roll, attempt a DC 15 Will save. On a failure, suffer a minor divine judgment effect (such as being dazzled for 1 round, taking 1d6 mental damage, or being frightened 1).  
Marked for Judgment 2: The DC of your Will save increases to 18, and the effects become more severe (such as being stunned for 1 round, taking 2d6 mental damage, or being frightened 2). The trigger expands to natural rolls of 1-2.  
Marked for Judgment 3: The DC of your Will save increases to 20, and the effects become severe (such as being paralyzed for 1 round, taking 3d6 mental damage, or being frightened 3). The trigger expands to natural rolls of 1-3. Righteous NPCs may refuse to interact with you, and some divine abilities may automatically fail when used on you.  
Acquisition: Gain the Marked for Judgment condition when your Corruption Points reach 5 or higher. The level equals your CP divided by 5 (rounded down, maximum 3).  
Removal: The Marked for Judgment condition is removed when your CP drops below 5\. This typically requires significant repentance, atonement, or divine intervention.  
Antediluvian Flavor: A visible mark of judgment appears on your body—a brand of fire, a shadow that never leaves, or a spiritual stench that righteous beings can smell. You feel the weight of divine attention, as if the Most High is watching your every move, waiting for the moment of judgment. Natural disasters may seem to target you specifically, and righteous prophecies may mention your name as an example of corruption.  
Example: Keturah has 8 CP. She gains the Marked for Judgment 1 condition. During a battle with a Nephilim warlord, she rolls a natural 1 on her attack roll. She must attempt a DC 15 Will save. She rolls a 9 on the d20, plus her \+3 modifier, for a total of 12—failure. She is dazzled for 1 round, her vision clouded by a vision of the coming Flood and her place in the judgment to come.  
Watcher-Marked  
Level: Variable (1-3)  
Traits: Divine, Neutral  
Effect: You bear the visible mark of a fallen Watcher's influence, granting power at the cost of spiritual danger.  
Mechanical Effects:  
Watcher-Marked 1: You can cast one cantrip from your patron Watcher's tradition as an occult innate spell once per day. However, you gain 1 CP whenever you use this ability.  
Watcher-Marked 2: You can cast one 1st-level spell from your patron Watcher's tradition as an occult innate spell once per day. However, you gain 1 CP whenever you use this ability, and you take a \-1 penalty to saves against possession attempts.  
Watcher-Marked 3: You can cast one 2nd-level spell from your patron Watcher's tradition as an occult innate spell once per day. However, you gain 2 CP whenever you use this ability, and you take a \-2 penalty to saves against possession attempts. Disembodied Refa'im spirits and other corrupted beings can sense your mark and may seek you out.  
Acquisition: Gain the Watcher-Marked condition when you willingly accept a Watcher's patronage or when you study forbidden texts extensively. The level increases as you deepen your connection to the Watcher.  
Removal: The Watcher-Marked condition can only be removed through divine intervention, powerful exorcism, or the death of your patron Watcher (which is impossible until the final judgment). Attempting to remove the mark without proper preparation may result in severe consequences, including possession or spiritual destruction.  
Antediluvian Flavor: A visible mark appears on your body—a sigil of fire, a pattern of stars, or a brand that glows with unnatural light. The mark may change appearance based on your patron Watcher (Azazel's mark resembles a sword, Semyaza's an enchanted symbol, Baraqel's a constellation). You feel a connection to your patron Watcher, receiving dreams, visions, or whispers that guide your actions. Other Watcher-marked individuals can recognize your mark and may seek you out as allies or rivals.  
Example: Keturah has studied Azazel's teachings extensively and bears his mark on her forehead—a bronze sigil that glows when she uses forbidden magic. She has the Watcher-Marked 2 condition, allowing her to cast Magic Weapon once per day. When she uses this ability to enhance her bronze dagger before a battle, the sigil glows with fiery light, and she gains 1 CP. Later, a disembodied Refa'im spirit senses her mark and attempts to possess her, taking advantage of her vulnerability to spiritual influence.  
Sample Encounter: Nephilim Ambush  
Setup  
The party travels through a narrow canyon on their way to a hidden Sethite enclave. Unbeknownst to them, a Nephilim patrol has been tracking them for hours, using the oversized environment to conceal their approach.  
Nephilim Patrol:  
1 Nephilim Scout (Level 8\)  
2 Nephilim Thralls (Level 5 each)  
Environment:  
Narrow canyon with 30-foot-high walls  
Scattered boulders and fallen trees providing cover  
A dry riverbed running through the center  
Limited visibility due to shadows and rock formations  
Round 1: Surprise  
Initiative: The GM calls for Stealth checks from the Nephilim and Perception checks from the party.  
Nephilim Scout: Stealth \+16, rolls 14, total Nephilim Thralls: Stealth \+12, roll 11 and 15, totals 23 and Tamar (Hunter): Perception \+14, rolls 16, total 30—NOTICES the scout's shadow  
Kedar (Warrior): Perception \+10, rolls 8, total 18—FAILS to notice  
Enoch (Priest): Perception \+12, rolls 12, total 24—NOTICES the thralls' movement  
Keturah (Magi): Perception \+9, rolls 6, total 15—FAILS to notice  
Rahab (Gibbor): Perception \+11, rolls 13, total 24—NOTICES the thralls' movement  
Surprise Results: Kedar and Keturah are surprised. Tamar, Enoch, and Rahab are not surprised.  
Nephilim Scout's Turn (Initiative 30): The scout emerges from behind a massive boulder, its 14-foot frame casting a long shadow across the canyon. It spends all three actions to Strike Kedar with its bronze greatsword. Attack roll: \+18, rolls 11, total 29\. Kedar's AC is 22—HIT\! Damage: 2d12+7 slashing, rolls 8 and 6, total 21 damage. Kedar is wounded but still standing.  
Nephilim Thrall 1's Turn (Initiative 27): The thrall moves from behind a fallen tree and Strikes Keturah with its bronze spear. Attack roll: \+14, rolls 17, total 31\. Keturah's AC is 16—HIT\! Damage: 1d8+5 piercing, rolls 5, total 10 damage. Keturah is injured and realizes she's under attack.  
Nephilim Thrall 2's Turn (Initiative 23): The thrall moves to flank Kedar and Strikes with its battleaxe. Attack roll: \+14, rolls 9, total 23\. Kedar's AC is 22—HIT\! Damage: 1d10+5 slashing, rolls 7, total 12 damage. Kedar is now seriously wounded.  
Tamar's Turn (Initiative 24): Tamar is not surprised. She spends one action to shout a warning to her allies: "Nephilim ambush\! Take cover\!" She spends her second action to Stride behind a boulder for cover. She spends her third action to draw her composite longbow.  
Enoch's Turn (Initiative 24): Enoch is not surprised. He spends one action to move toward Kedar to provide support. He spends his second action to cast Shield of Faith on Kedar. The spell requires a somatic component (raising his hands in prayer) and a material component (a small clay vial of blessed water). The spell succeeds, granting Kedar a \+1 status bonus to AC for 1 minute. Enoch spends his third action to present his holy symbol and use his Turn Unholy ability. The Nephilim thralls must attempt Will saves (DC 22). Thrall 1 rolls a 9, total 17—FAILURE, frightened 1\. Thrall 2 rolls a 14, total 20—SUCCESS, unaffected.  
Rahab's Turn (Initiative 24): Rahab is not surprised. She spends one action to Stride toward the Nephilim scout. She spends her second action to use her Giant's Grip feat to wield her bronze greatsword one-handed. She spends her third action to Strike the scout. Attack roll: \+16, rolls 18, total 34—CRITICAL HIT\! Damage: 2d12+6 slashing, rolls 7 and 11, total 34 damage. The scout is severely wounded.  
Kedar's Turn (Initiative 18): Kedar is no longer surprised. He is wounded (21 \+ 12 \= 33 damage taken) but still standing. He spends one action to raise his shield, gaining \+2 AC against the scout's next attack. He spends his second action to Step 5 feet to get a better angle on the scout. He spends his third action to Strike the scout with his bronze greatsword. Attack roll: \+16, rolls 13, total 29—HIT\! Damage: 1d12+5 slashing, rolls 9, total 14 damage. The scout is now critically wounded.  
Keturah's Turn (Initiative 15): Keturah is no longer surprised. She is injured (10 damage taken) and realizes the danger. She could use forbidden magic to fight back, but she remembers her recent corruption and decides to act righteously. She spends one action to Stride behind Tamar for cover. She spends her second action to draw her dagger. She spends her third action to use the Aid action to grant Tamar a \+1 circumstance bonus to her next attack roll. This is a righteous choice—she could have used magic to attack, but instead she supports her ally without risking corruption.  
Round 2: The Battle Continues  
Nephilim Scout's Turn (Initiative 30): The scout is critically wounded but still dangerous. It spends one action to Step away from Rahab. It spends its second action to Strike Kedar again. Attack roll: \+18, rolls 6, total 24—HIT\! Damage: 2d12+7 slashing, rolls 4 and 3, total 14 damage. Kedar is now near death. The scout spends its third action to attempt a Demoralize against Kedar. Intimidation check: \+14, rolls 15, total 29\. Kedar's Will save: \+10, rolls 11, total 21—FAILURE, frightened 1\.  
Nephilim Thrall 1's Turn (Initiative 27): The thrall is frightened 1 from Enoch's Turn Unholy. It spends all three actions to Step away from Enoch, moving toward the canyon exit.  
Nephilim Thrall 2's Turn (Initiative 23): The thrall is unaffected by Turn Unholy. It spends one action to Step toward Keturah. It spends its second action to Strike Keturah with its battleaxe. Attack roll: \+14, rolls 12, total 26—HIT\! Damage: 1d10+5 slashing, rolls 8, total 13 damage. Keturah is now seriously wounded. The thrall spends its third action to attempt a Demoralize against Keturah. Intimidation check: \+12, rolls 10, total 22\. Keturah's Will save: \+8, rolls 14, total 22—TIE, success. Keturah resists the fear.  
Tamar's Turn (Initiative 24): Tamar has the Aid bonus from Keturah (+1 circumstance bonus to her next attack). She spends one action to Stride to a better position. She spends her second action to draw an arrow. She spends her third action to Strike the Nephilim scout. Attack roll: \+16, rolls 19, total 35—CRITICAL HIT\! Damage: 1d8+4 piercing, rolls 6, total 10 damage. The scout is now dead, its massive form collapsing to the canyon floor with a thunderous crash.  
Enoch's Turn (Initiative 24): Enoch sees that Kedar is near death and Keturah is seriously wounded. He spends one action to Stride toward Kedar. He spends his second action to cast Healing Word on Kedar. The spell requires a verbal component (speaking words of healing) and a material component (a drop of blessed water). The spell succeeds, restoring 3d8+5 Hit Points to Kedar. Enoch rolls 3, 5, and 6 on the dice, total 14, plus 5, total 19 Hit Points restored. Kedar is now stable. Enoch spends his third action to present his holy symbol again and use Turn Unholy on the remaining thralls. Thrall 1 is already fleeing. Thrall 2 must attempt another Will save (DC 22). Rolls 8, total 16—FAILURE, frightened 1\.  
Rahab's Turn (Initiative 24): Rahab sees that the scout is dead and turns her attention to the thralls. She spends one action to Stride toward Thrall 2\. She spends her second action to Strike Thrall 2 with her greatsword. Attack roll: \+16, rolls 14, total 30—HIT\! Damage: 2d12+6 slashing, rolls 5 and 9, total 20 damage. Thrall 2 is now dead.  
Kedar's Turn (Initiative 18): Kedar is stable but frightened 1\. He spends one action to Step away from the dead scout. He spends his second action to lower his shield. He spends his third action to use the Aid action to grant Rahab a \+1 circumstance bonus to her next attack roll.  
Keturah's Turn (Initiative 15): Keturah is seriously wounded but stable. She sees that Thrall 1 is fleeing. She could use forbidden magic to stop it, but she chooses righteousness again. She spends one action to call out to Tamar: "Let it go\! We've won\!" She spends her second action to sheathe her dagger. She spends her third action to use Medicine to stabilize her own wounds. Medicine check: \+7, rolls 15, total 22\. DC 15—SUCCESS\! She stops the bleeding and prevents further damage.  
Aftermath  
The battle is over. The Nephilim scout and one thrall are dead. The other thrall has fled. The party is wounded but victorious.  
Soul Economy Consequences:  
Kedar: Fought bravely to protect his allies. Gains 1 RP for righteous combat.  
Tamar: Used her skills effectively and showed no unnecessary cruelty. Gains 1 RP for competent and righteous action.  
Enoch: Used divine miracles to heal and protect, and turned away corrupted beings. Gains 2 RP for righteous service.  
Rahab: Fought with strength and purpose, protecting her allies without unnecessary violence. Gains 1 RP for righteous combat.  
Keturah: Resisted the temptation to use forbidden magic twice, choosing to support her allies through mundane means. Gains 2 RP for resisting corruption.  
Loot and Rewards:  
The Nephilim scout carries a bronze greatsword (worth 50 gp), a pouch with 25 gp, and a clay tablet with patrol routes.  
The dead thrall carries a bronze spear (worth 5 gp), a waterskin, and rations for three days.  
The fleeing thrall drops its battleaxe (worth 10 gp) in its haste to escape.  
Environmental Consequences:  
The noise of battle may attract other Nephilim patrols or corrupted wildlife.  
The dead Nephilim's spirit (Refa'im) may linger, potentially possessing someone or haunting the area.  
The canyon is now safe for the party to rest and recover, but they should move on quickly to avoid further encounters.  
This encounter demonstrates how the three-action economy, combat mechanics, and Soul Economy interact in actual gameplay. Each character's choices—both tactical and moral—affect the outcome of the battle and their spiritual condition. The integration of PF2e mechanics with antediluvian flavor creates a rich, immersive experience that captures the unique challenges and themes of the Nephilim Wars setting.
